## Daniel - Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_txw3)

Introduction to Daniel
======================

Part 1: General Introduction
----------------------------

### Outline of the book of Daniel

1. Daniel and his friends resolve not to defile themselves (1:1–21\)
2. Daniel interprets Nebuchadnezzar’s dream about a statue (2:1–49\)
3. God delivers Daniel’s friends from the fiery furnace (3:1–30\)
4. Daniel interprets Nebuchadnezzar’s dream about a tree (4:1–37\)
5. Daniel interprets the writing on the wall at Belshazzar’s feast (5:1–31\)
6. God keeps Daniel safe in the den of lions (6:1–28\)
7. Daniel’s vision of four beasts (7:1–28\)
8. Daniel’s vision of a ram and a goat (8:1–27\)
9. Daniel’s prayer for the restoration of Jerusalem and Gabriel’s explanation (9:1–27\)
10. Daniel’s vision of an angel and the kings of the north and south (10:1–12:13\)

    1. Daniel has a vision and a man appears to him to explain it (10:1–11:1\)
        2. The wars between the kings of the south and north (11:2–45\)
        3. What will happen at “the time of the end” (12:1–13\)

### What is the Book of Daniel about?

The first part of the book of Daniel (chapters 1–6\) relates stories about four young men whom the Babylonians took from the kingdom of Judah as prisoners. One of these men was named Daniel. He had three friends, to whom the Babylonians gave the names Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego. These stories tell how these young men were faithful to God while living in a pagan land and serving a pagan king. They tell how God protected them and rewarded them because they were faithful. They also tell how God gave Daniel the ability to interpret dreams and explain mysteries.

The second part of the book of Daniel (chapters 7–12\) relates several prophetic visions that Daniel had. These chapters also describe, in several cases, the glorious appearance of angelic messengers who explained to Daniel what his visions meant.

### What title should translators use for this book?

Translators can use the traditional title “The book of Daniel” or just “Daniel.” They might also call this book “Stories and Visions of Daniel” or “What Daniel Did and What God Showed Him.”

### Who wrote the Book of Daniel?

The first six chapters of the book speak of Daniel and his friends in the third person (as “he” and “they”). This suggests that someone else, perhaps another Judean who was exiled in Babylon with them, may have written these chapters to record how they remained faithful to God and how God protected them.

In the last six chapters of the book, Daniel speaks of himself in the first person (saying “I, Daniel”). (The seventh chapter serves as a transition; it begins by speaking of Daniel in the third person, but this introduces an account by Daniel himself that ends with this first\-person attestation.) This indicates that Daniel himself wrote this part of the book.

Part 2: Important Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------------------------

(Some material may go here.)

Part 3: Important Translation Issues
------------------------------------

### The meaning of Daniel’s visions

Even though angelic messengers explain to Daniel what his visions mean, it is not entirely clear to modern readers what historic time periods they predicted or whether they predict things that will happen at the end of the world. It is not necessary to come to conclusions about this in order to translate the book. The language in the visions is straightforward and descriptive, and translation teams should be able to reflect successfully in their languages what it says. What it means is a matter for preachers and teachers of the Scriptures to try to understand and explain.

### The original languages of the book of Daniel

Half of the book of Daniel is written in Hebrew, and the other half of the book is written in Aramaic. These halves do not correspond with the two parts that are stories and visions. Instead, chapters 1 and 8–12 are in Hebrew, while chapters 2–7 are in Aramaic. This means that the first chapter of each part of the book is in a different language from the rest of that part. In the first part, the Hebrew chapter introduces a transition from the young men’s former life in Jerusalem to their life in the Babylonian and Persian courts, where Aramaic was spoken. In the second part, the Aramaic chapter introduces a transition from the affairs of those foreign courts to matters that affect the Jewish people more directly. Daniel and his three friends were native speakers of Hebrew who had to learn Aramaic (1:4\), so they or others who were with them would have been able to put together a book like this that used both languages.

Even in the English ULT text, some of the differences between the original Hebrew and Aramaic texts will be evident. For example, translators may already have encountered the common Hebrew expression “answered and said” in their work on other books. In the Aramaic sections of Daniel, the equivalent expression is “answered and was saying.” It is not necessary to try to show such differences in your translation. Consistently say things in the way that is most natural and meaningful in your translation.

### “all before that” and “all before this”

In the Aramaic portion of the book (chapters 2–7\), these similar phrases describe some relationship between what follows and what precedes. They do not indicate that what precedes happened before what follows. Their meaning is flexible and varies by context. Notes will suggest what they mean and how you might translate them in each instance.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 0:0


## Daniel - Chapter 1 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_ln8b)

Introduction to Daniel
======================

Part 1: General Introduction
----------------------------

### Outline of the book of Daniel

1. Daniel and his friends resolve not to defile themselves (1:1–21\)
2. Daniel interprets Nebuchadnezzar’s dream about a statue (2:1–49\)
3. God delivers Daniel’s friends from the fiery furnace (3:1–30\)
4. Daniel interprets Nebuchadnezzar’s dream about a tree (4:1–37\)
5. Daniel interprets the writing on the wall at Belshazzar’s feast (5:1–31\)
6. God keeps Daniel safe in the den of lions (6:1–28\)
7. Daniel’s vision of four beasts (7:1–28\)
8. Daniel’s vision of a ram and a goat (8:1–27\)
9. Daniel’s prayer for the restoration of Jerusalem and Gabriel’s explanation (9:1–27\)
10. Daniel’s vision of an angel and the kings of the north and south (10:1–12:13\)

    1. Daniel has a vision and a man appears to him to explain it (10:1–11:1\)
        2. The wars between the kings of the south and north (11:2–45\)
        3. What will happen at “the time of the end” (12:1–13\)

### What is the Book of Daniel about?

The first part of the book of Daniel (chapters 1–6\) relates stories about four young men whom the Babylonians took from the kingdom of Judah as prisoners. One of these men was named Daniel. The second part of the book of Daniel (chapters 7–12\) relates several prophetic visions that Daniel had. These chapters also describe, in several cases, the glorious appearance of angelic messengers who explained to Daniel what his visions meant.

The first six chapters of the book speak of Daniel and his friends in the third person (as “he” and “they”). This indicates that Daniel himself wrote this part of the book.

Part 2: Important Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------------------------

Part 3: Important Translation Issues
------------------------------------

### The meaning of Daniel’s visions

Even though angelic messengers explain to Daniel what his visions mean, it is not entirely clear to modern readers what historic time periods they predicted or whether they predict things that will happen at the end of the world. It is not necessary to come to conclusions about this in order to translate the book. The language in the visions is straightforward and descriptive, and translation teams should be able to reflect successfully in their languages what it says. What it means is a matter for preachers and teachers of the Scriptures to try to understand and explain.

### The original languages of the book of Daniel

Half of the book of Daniel is written in Hebrew, and the other half of the book is written in Aramaic. These halves do not correspond with the two parts that are stories and visions. Instead, chapters 1 and 8–12 are in Hebrew, while chapters 2–7 are in Aramaic. This means that the first chapter of each part of the book is in a different language from the rest of that part. In the first part, the Hebrew chapter introduces a transition from the young men’s former life in Jerusalem to their life in the Babylonian and Persian courts, where Aramaic was spoken. In the second part, the Aramaic chapter introduces a transition from the affairs of those foreign courts to matters that affect the Jewish people more directly. Even in the English ULT text, some of the differences between the original Hebrew and Aramaic texts will be evident. For example, translators may already have encountered the common Hebrew expression “answered and said” in their work on other books. In the Aramaic sections of Daniel, the equivalent expression is “answered and was saying.” It is not necessary to try to show such differences in your translation. Consistently say things in the way that is most natural and meaningful in your translation.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:0


## Daniel 1:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_lmrl)

בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֣ת שָׁל֔וֹשׁ

**"In the year of three"**

If your language would not represent a number in a form such as **three**, you could represent this number in a form that your language would use. In this verse, the number identifies the third year of the reign of Jehoiakim. Alternate translation: \[In the third year of the reign]

See: Ordinal Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ordinal Numbers (ID: `33393@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jehoiakim (ID: `person:Jehoiakim`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Daniel 1:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_cg2c)

וַ⁠יִּתֵּן֩ אֲדֹנָ֨⁠י בְּ⁠יָד֜⁠וֹ

**"And the Lord gave into his hand"**

Here, **hand** represents “power” or “control.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And the Lord gave into his control]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Shinar (ID: `place:Shinar`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Jehoiakim (ID: `person:Jehoiakim`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

לְ⁠אַשְׁפְּנַ֖ז

## Daniel 1:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_s1nj)

מִ⁠בְּנֵ֧י יִשְׂרָאֵ֛ל

**"from the sons of Israel"**

The author is speaking as if all the people descended from the man Israel were his **sons**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[from the people of Israel]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ashpenaz (ID: `person:Ashpenaz`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Daniel 1:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_pd8c)

וּ⁠מִ⁠זֶּ֥רַע הַ⁠מְּלוּכָ֖ה

**"both from the seed of the kingdom"**

The author is speaking as if the members of the royal family were **seed** that the kingdom had produced. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[both from the royal family]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ashpenaz (ID: `person:Ashpenaz`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Daniel 1:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_l2iw)

וּ⁠מִ⁠זֶּ֥רַע הַ⁠מְּלוּכָ֖ה

**"both from the seed of the kingdom"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **kingdom**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[both from the sons of the royal family]

## Daniel 1:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_kpht)

וְ⁠יֹ֤דְעֵי דַ֨עַת֙ וּ⁠מְבִינֵ֣י מַדָּ֔ע

**"and knowers of knowledge and understanders of knowledge"**

For poetic purposes, the author is using two different words that have basically the same meaning. The ULT translates each one as **knowledge** because English does not have two different words that it can use in this context. But if your language does, it would be appropriate to use them here. Alternate translation: \[knowers of knowledge and understanders of information]

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chaldeans (ID: `group:Chaldea`); Have Insight (ID: `keyterm:HaveInsight`); Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`); Intention (ID: `keyterm:Intention`); Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Chaldea (ID: `place:Chaldea`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`)

## Daniel 1:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_tevh)

וְ⁠יֹ֤דְעֵי דַ֨עַת֙

**"and knowers of knowledge"**

The author is using a construction in which a verb and its object come from the same root. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this.

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

וּ⁠לְשׁ֥וֹן כַּשְׂדִּֽים

**"and tongue of the Chaldeans"**

Here, **tongue** represents “language.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and language of the Chaldeans]

## Daniel 1:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_oj50)

בְּ⁠כָל־חָכְמָ֗ה וְ⁠יֹ֤דְעֵי דַ֨עַת֙ וּ⁠מְבִינֵ֣י מַדָּ֔ע

**"in all wisdom and knowers of knowledge and understanders of knowledge"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **wisdom** and **knowledge**, you could express the same ideas in another way. Alternate translation: \[in everything wise and who knew many things and who understood many things]

## Daniel 1:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_qu3f)

דְּבַר־י֣וֹם בְּ⁠יוֹמ֗⁠וֹ

**"the matter of a day in its day"**

The author is using **the matter of a day in its day** as a common expression of the culture to mean “a set amount for each day.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[a set amount for each day]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Daniel 1:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_yaxs)

מִ⁠פַּת־בַּ֤ג הַ⁠מֶּ֨לֶךְ֙ וּ⁠מִ⁠יֵּ֣ין מִשְׁתָּ֔י⁠ו

**"from the portion of the king and from the wine of his drinking"**

Daniel is using this possessive form to mean the food and wine that the king himself eats and drinks. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[from the same food that the king eats and the same wine that he drinks]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Daniel 1:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_h8mk)

וּ⁠מִ֨⁠קְצָתָ֔⁠ם יַֽעַמְד֖וּ

**"and from the end of them they would stand"**

The pronoun **them** refers to the three years of the young men’s training, while the pronoun **they** refers to the young men. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and at the end of those three years the young men would serve]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Daniel 1:5 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_w7q0)

לִ⁠פְנֵ֥י הַ⁠מֶּֽלֶךְ

**"to the face of the king"**

Here, **to the face of** represents being in front of or in the presence of the king. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[before the king]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Daniel 1:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ez12)

דָּנִיֵּ֣אל חֲנַנְיָ֔ה מִֽישָׁאֵ֖ל וַ⁠עֲזַרְיָֽה

**"Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah"**

The author is introducing **Daniel**, **Hananiah**, **Mishael**, and **Azariah** as new participants in the story. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[four young men whose names were Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah]

See: Introduction of New and Old Participants

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:6
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of New and Old Participants (ID: `33421@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`)

## Daniel 1:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_lxqi)

בֵּ֣לְטְשַׁאצַּ֗ר וְ⁠לַֽ⁠חֲנַנְיָה֙ שַׁדְרַ֔ךְ וּ⁠לְ⁠מִֽישָׁאֵ֣ל מֵישַׁ֔ךְ וְ⁠לַ⁠עֲזַרְיָ֖ה עֲבֵ֥ד נְגֽוֹ

**"Belteshazzar; and to Hananiah, Shadrach; and to Mishael, Meshach; and to Azariah, Abednego"**

The words **Belteshazzar**, **Shadrach**, **Meshach**, and **Abednego** are the Babylonian names of these young men.

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:7
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_exiv)

וְ⁠לַֽ⁠חֲנַנְיָה֙ שַׁדְרַ֔ךְ וּ⁠לְ⁠מִֽישָׁאֵ֣ל מֵישַׁ֔ךְ וְ⁠לַ⁠עֲזַרְיָ֖ה עֲבֵ֥ד נְגֽוֹ

**"and to Hananiah, Shadrach; and to Mishael, Meshach; and to Azariah, Abednego"**

The author is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and to Hananiah, he gave the name Shadrach; and to Mishael, he gave the name Meshach; and to Azariah, he gave the name Abednego]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:7
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_watk)

וַ⁠יָּ֤שֶׂם דָּנִיֵּאל֙ עַל־לִבּ֔⁠וֹ

**"But Daniel set upon his heart"**

The author is using **set upon his heart** as a common expression of the culture to mean “resolved” or “firmly decided.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[But Daniel firmly decided]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Defile (ID: `keyterm:Stain`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Daniel 1:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ivba)

וַ⁠יִּתֵּ֤ן הָֽ⁠אֱלֹהִים֙ אֶת־דָּ֣נִיֵּ֔אל לְ⁠חֶ֖סֶד וּֽ⁠לְ⁠רַחֲמִ֑ים

**"And God gave Daniel to favor and to compassions"**

The author is using **gave Daniel to favor and to compassions** as a common expression of the culture to mean “caused the chief of the eunuchs to regard Daniel with favor and kindness.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And God caused Daniel to be regarded with favor and compassion]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:9
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_h21g)

לְ⁠חֶ֖סֶד וּֽ⁠לְ⁠רַחֲמִ֑ים

**"to favor and to compassions"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **favor** and **compassions**, you could express the same ideas in another way. Alternate translation: \[to be regarded favorably and compassionately]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:9
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 1:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_zhvy)

לִ⁠פְנֵ֖י

**"to the face of"**

Here, **to the face of** represents being in front of the chief of the eunuchs. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[in the presence of] or \[in front of]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_f6yg)

יָרֵ֤א אֲנִי֙

**"I, I fear"**

The chief of the eunuchs is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **fear** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that he himself is afraid of what the king might do to him. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[I myself fear my lord the king] or \[for my part, I fear]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Public Official (ID: `person:GenericOfficial`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_fr83)

אֲשֶׁ֡ר לָ⁠מָּה֩ יִרְאֶ֨ה אֶת־פְּנֵי⁠כֶ֜ם זֹֽעֲפִ֗ים מִן־הַ⁠יְלָדִים֙ אֲשֶׁ֣ר כְּ⁠גִֽילְ⁠כֶ֔ם

**"For why should he see your faces looking worse than the youths who {are} like your age"**

The chief of the eunuchs is using the question form to express his fear of what the king might do to him if the young men looked unhealthy. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[For I am afraid that he will see your faces looking worse than your peers!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Public Official (ID: `person:GenericOfficial`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_gxj1)

וְ⁠חִיַּבְתֶּ֥ם אֶת־רֹאשִׁ֖⁠י לַ⁠מֶּֽלֶךְ

**"Then you would endanger my head with the king"**

The chief of the eunuchs is using **endanger my head with the king** as a common expression of the culture to mean “cause the king to have me executed.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Then you would cause the king to have me beheaded]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Public Official (ID: `person:GenericOfficial`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_vegz)

אֲשֶׁ֡ר לָ⁠מָּה֩ יִרְאֶ֨ה אֶת־פְּנֵי⁠כֶ֜ם זֹֽעֲפִ֗ים מִן־הַ⁠יְלָדִים֙ אֲשֶׁ֣ר כְּ⁠גִֽילְ⁠כֶ֔ם וְ⁠חִיַּבְתֶּ֥ם אֶת־רֹאשִׁ֖⁠י לַ⁠מֶּֽלֶךְ

**"For why should he see your faces looking worse than the youths who {are} like your age? Then you would endanger my head with the king"**

The word **you** is plural here because the chief of the eunuchs is addressing Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah together, so use the plural form in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

See: Forms of ‘You’ — Singular

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Forms of ‘You’ — Singular (ID: `33321@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Public Official (ID: `person:GenericOfficial`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ufzm)

הַ⁠מֶּלְצַ֑ר

**"the steward"**

A **steward** is a kind of official who was put in charge of overseeing and caring for others. If your readers would not be familiar with what a steward is, you could use the name of a similar person that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[the caretaker] or \[the overseer]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:11
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Melzar (ID: `person:Melzar`); Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 1:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ztyd)

אֶת־עֲבָדֶ֖י⁠ךָ

**"your servants"**

Daniel is referring to himself and his companions in the third person as **your servants** to show respect and humility before the steward. If it would be helpful in your language, you could express this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[us]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:12
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 1:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_jzmc)

וְ⁠יִתְּנוּ־לָ֜⁠נוּ

**"and let them give to us"**

Daniel is using the pronoun **them** in a general sense, not to refer to specific people. Use an expression for this that would be natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and let us be given some vegetables to eat]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:12
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 1:12 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_idtd)

וְ⁠יִתְּנוּ־לָ֜⁠נוּ מִן־הַ⁠זֵּרֹעִ֛ים וְ⁠נֹאכְלָ֖ה וּ⁠מַ֥יִם וְ⁠נִשְׁתֶּֽה

**"and let them give to us from vegetables, and let us eat, and water, and let us drink"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. He means that he wants the steward to give them vegetables to eat and water to drink. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and let them give to us vegetables to eat and water to drink]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:12
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 1:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_y9s8)

וְ⁠יֵרָא֤וּ לְ⁠פָנֶ֨י⁠ךָ֙ מַרְאֵ֔י⁠נוּ וּ⁠מַרְאֵה֙ הַ⁠יְלָדִ֔ים

**"Then let our appearances be seen to your face, and the appearance of the youths"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **appearances** and **appearance**, you could express the same ideas in other ways. Alternate translation: \[Then call us before you to see how we look and how the youths look after they have been]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:13
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 1:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_yer6)

נִרְאָ֤ה מַרְאֵי⁠הֶם֙

**"their appearances, it was seen"**

The expression **it was seen** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[their faces looked]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:15
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 1:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_q9mw)

וּ⁠בְרִיאֵ֖י בָּשָׂ֑ר

**"and fatter of flesh"**

The author is using **fatter of flesh** as a common expression of the culture to mean “healthier” or “better nourished.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and better nourished]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:15
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 1:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_lyc7)

וְ⁠הַ⁠יְלָדִ֤ים הָ⁠אֵ֨לֶּה֙ אַרְבַּעְתָּ֔⁠ם נָתַ֨ן לָ⁠הֶ֧ם הָֽ⁠אֱלֹהִ֛ים

**"And these four youths, God gave to them"**

The author is stating the topic **these four youths** at the beginning of the sentence and then referring back to them with the pronoun **them**. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about the four youths together with the information about what God gave to them. Alternate translation: \[And God gave these four youths]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 1:17
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

לִ⁠פְנֵ֖י נְבֻכַדְנֶצַּֽר

**"to the face of Nebuchadnezzar"**

Here, **to the face of** represents being in front of Nebuchadnezzar. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[into the presence of Nebuchadnezzar] or \[in front of Nebuchadnezzar]

וְ⁠לֹ֤א נִמְצָא֙

**"and none was found"**

מִ⁠כֻּלָּ֔⁠ם

**"from all of them"**

דְּבַר֙ חָכְמַ֣ת בִּינָ֔ה

**"matter of wisdom {and} understanding"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **wisdom** and **understanding**, you could express the same ideas in another way. Alternate translation: \[matter requiring them to be wise and to understand well]

## Daniel 1:20 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_e2ae)

עֶ֣שֶׂר יָד֗וֹת עַ֤ל כָּל

**"ten hands above all of"**

The author is using **ten hands above** as a common expression of the culture to mean “ten times better than.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[ten times better than all of]

## Daniel 1:20 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_qa7v)

הַֽ⁠חַרְטֻמִּים֙ הָֽ⁠אַשָּׁפִ֔ים

**"the magicians {and} enchanters"**

The terms **magicians** and **enchanters** describe people who claimed to have secret knowledge and the ability to perform magic and interpret dreams and omens. If your readers would not be familiar with what these people did, you could use the names of similar people that your readers would recognize, or you could use more general terms. Alternate translation: \[the people who practiced magic and claimed secret knowledge]

עַד־שְׁנַ֥ת אַחַ֖ת לְ⁠כ֥וֹרֶשׁ הַ⁠מֶּֽלֶךְ

**"until the year of one to Cyrus the king"**

If your language would not represent a number in a form such as **one**, you could represent this number in a form that your language would use. Alternate translation: \[until the first year that Cyrus was king]

## Daniel - Chapter 2 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_zznb)

Daniel 2 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Nebuchadnezzar has a troubling dream that he cannot remember (1\)
2. His advisors fail to tell him the dream, so he orders their execution (2–13\)
3. Daniel appeals for time, and God shows him the dream (14–19\)
4. Daniel praises God (20–23\)
5. Daniel tells Nebuchadnezzar his dream and its meaning (24–45\)
6. Nebuchadnezzar rewards Daniel and his friends (46–49\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in Daniel’s prayer of thanksgiving in 2:20–23\.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:0


## Daniel 2:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_mzp8)

וּ⁠בִ⁠שְׁנַ֣ת שְׁתַּ֗יִם

**"And in the year of two"**

The expression **year of two** uses a cardinal number where your language might use an ordinal number, since it refers to the second year of the reign of Nebuchadnezzar. If your language would not represent a number in a form such as **two**, you could represent this number in a form that your language would use. Alternate translation: \[In the second year]

See: Ordinal Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ordinal Numbers (ID: `33393@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 2:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_nygq)

חָלַ֥ם נְבֻֽכַדְנֶצַּ֖ר חֲלֹמ֑וֹת

**"Nebuchadnezzar dreamed dreams"**

Daniel is using a construction in which a verb and its object come from the same root. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this. Alternate translation: \[Nebuchadnezzar dreamed]

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 2:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_prdw)

חָלַ֥ם נְבֻֽכַדְנֶצַּ֖ר חֲלֹמ֑וֹת וַ⁠תִּתְפָּ֣עֶם רוּח֔⁠וֹ

**"Nebuchadnezzar dreamed dreams. And his spirit was troubled"**

The expression **his spirit was troubled** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And this troubled his spirit]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 2:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_y91v)

וּ⁠שְׁנָת֖⁠וֹ נִהְיְתָ֥ה עָלָֽי⁠ו

**"and his sleep finished for him"**

Daniel is using **his sleep finished for him** as a common expression of the culture to mean that Nebuchadnezzar was unable to sleep. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and he could not sleep]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_fk7p)

לַֽ⁠חַרְטֻמִּ֜ים וְ⁠לָֽ⁠אַשָּׁפִ֗ים וְ⁠לַֽ⁠מְכַשְּׁפִים֙

**"the magicians and the enchanters and the sorcerers"**

The terms **magicians**, **enchanters**, and **sorcerers** are different kinds of people who were thought to have special knowledge or powers, such as interpreting dreams, using magic, or communicating with spirits. If your readers would not be familiar with what these are, you could use the names of similar people that your readers would recognize, or you could use more general terms. Alternate translation: \[the users of magic and the spell\-casters and the fortune\-tellers]

See: Translate Unknowns

## Daniel 2:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ukqy)

The Chaldeans are using **live forever** as a common expression of the culture to mean “may you live for a long time.” It was a respectful way to greet a king. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Daniel 2:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xn90)

מַלְכָּ֕⁠א חֶלְמָ֛⁠א יֵאמַ֥ר לְ⁠עַבְד֖וֹ⁠הִי

**"Let the king tell the dream to his servants"**

Even though the Chaldeans are addressing the king directly, they are speaking of him in the third person as **the king** and of themselves in the third person as **his servants** to show respect. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in a respectful form using the second person and the first person. Alternate translation: \[O king, please tell us your servants the dream]

See: First, Second or Third Person

## Daniel 2:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_yic2)

* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_to8z)

**"you are buying time"**

The king is using **you are buying time** as a common expression of the culture to mean “you are trying to gain more time.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[you are trying extend the time allotted]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:20 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_p4k3)

לֶהֱוֵ֨א שְׁמֵ֤⁠הּ דִּֽי־אֱלָהָ⁠א֙ מְבָרַ֔ךְ

**"May his name, of God, be blessed"**

David is saying what he wants to happen. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about this form of speech, and follow the decision you have made as a team about how speakers will express in such contexts what they want to happen. Alternate translation: \[I desire for God’s name to be blessed]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:20
* **Associated Articles:** Irregular Use of Tenses (ID: `33403@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:20 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_xhyc)

לֶהֱוֵ֨א שְׁמֵ֤⁠הּ דִּֽי־אֱלָהָ⁠א֙ מְבָרַ֔ךְ

**"May his name, of God, be blessed"**

The expression **be blessed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[May people bless the name of God]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:20
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:20 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_reyq)

לֶהֱוֵ֨א שְׁמֵ֤⁠הּ דִּֽי־אֱלָהָ⁠א֙ מְבָרַ֔ךְ

**"May his name, of God, be blessed"**

Daniel is using the term **name** to represent who God is or to refer to God’s reputation. Alternate translation: \[May God be blessed]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:20
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 2:20 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_a628)

שְׁמֵ֤⁠הּ דִּֽי־אֱלָהָ⁠א֙

**"his name, of God"**

“His name, of God” is a doubling of reference. The phrase names God twice, once by pronoun, “name,” and once explicitly as “God.” It might seem that the expression **his name, of God** contains extra information that would not be natural to express in your language. If so, you could shorten it. Alternate translation: \[May God be blessed]

See: Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:20
* **Associated Articles:** Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit (ID: `33274@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 2:20 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_iybl)

שְׁמֵ֤⁠הּ דִּֽי־אֱלָהָ⁠א֙

**"his name, of God"**

Here, **his name** represents God himself. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[God himself]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:20
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

**"wisdom and power"**

## Daniel 2:20 (#7) (id: en_tn_DAN_f3z6)

מִן־עָלְמָ֖⁠א וְ⁠עַ֣ד־עָלְמָ֑⁠א

**"from eternity and to eternity"**

Daniel is referring to all of time by naming two extremes, **from eternity** and **to eternity**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[for all time]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:20
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:21 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xiw2)

יָהֵ֤ב חָכְמְתָ⁠א֙ לְ⁠חַכִּימִ֔ין וּ⁠מַנְדְּעָ֖⁠א לְ⁠יָדְעֵ֥י בִינָֽה

**"the one giving wisdom to the wise and knowledge to the knowers of understanding"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[the one giving wisdom and understanding to those who are wise]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:21
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:21 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_hrc4)

לְ⁠חַכִּימִ֔ין

**"to the wise"**

Daniel is using the adjective **wise** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[to wise people] or \[to those who are wise]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:21
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:21 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_sbwu)

וּ⁠מַנְדְּעָ֖⁠א לְ⁠יָדְעֵ֥י בִינָֽה

**"and knowledge to the knowers of understanding"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **knowledge** and **understanding**, you could express the same ideas in other ways. Alternate translation: \[and enabling those who can discern to know things]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:21
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:21 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_bfg9)

יָהֵ֤ב חָכְמְתָ⁠א֙ לְ⁠חַכִּימִ֔ין וּ⁠מַנְדְּעָ֖⁠א לְ⁠יָדְעֵ֥י בִינָֽה

**"the one giving wisdom to the wise and knowledge to the knowers of understanding"**

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:21
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:22 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xqad)

עַמִּיקָתָ֖⁠א וּ⁠מְסַתְּרָתָ֑⁠א

**"the deep and the hidden"**

Daniel is using the plural adjectives **the deep** and **the hidden** as nouns to mean certain kinds of things. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate these adjectives with equivalent phrases. Alternate translation: \[deep things and hidden things]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:22
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:22 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_bp95)

עַמִּיקָתָ֖⁠א וּ⁠מְסַתְּרָתָ֑⁠א

**"the deep and the hidden"**

The terms **the deep** and **the hidden** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[things that are very difficult to know] or \[things that are very obscure]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:22
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:22 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_eovn)

יָדַע֙ מָ֣ה בַ⁠חֲשׁוֹכָ֔⁠א ו⁠נהיר⁠א עִמֵּ֥⁠הּ שְׁרֵֽא

**"he knows what {is} in the darkness, and the light is lodged with him"**

Daniel is speaking of hidden things as if they were **darkness** and of what God knows as if it were **light**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[he knows what is secret, and understanding resides with him]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:22
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:23 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_szc1)

אֱלָ֣הּ אֲבָהָתִ֗⁠י

**"God of my fathers"**

Daniel is speaking of his ancestors as if they were his **fathers**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[God of my ancestors]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:23
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:23 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ibvx)

דִּֽי־בְעֵ֣ינָא מִנָּ֔⁠ךְ

**"what we sought from you"**

By **we**, Daniel means himself and his friends but not the one to whom he is speaking, so use the exclusive form of that word in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

## Daniel 2:24 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_sa4m)

כָּ⁠ל־קֳבֵ֣ל דְּנָ֗ה

**"All before this"**

See the discussion of this phrase in the introduction to the book of Daniel. In this context, the author is using it to indicate that follows is the result of what he has just described. Alternate translation: \[Consequently]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:24
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Arioch (ID: `person:Arioch.2`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 2:25 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_iweu)

אֲמַר־לֵ֗⁠הּ דִּֽי

**"he said to him, that"**

The word **that** introduces a direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:25
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Arioch (ID: `person:Arioch.2`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 2:25 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_kxsq)

בְּנֵ֤י גָֽלוּתָ⁠א֙ דִּ֣י יְה֔וּד דִּ֥י

**"the sons of the captivity of Judah who"**

Arioch is using **the sons of the captivity of Judah** as a common expression of the culture to mean the people who had been taken away from Judah into exile. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the exiles from Judah who]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:25
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Arioch (ID: `person:Arioch.2`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

גָֽלוּתָ⁠א֙ דִּ֣י יְה֔וּד דִּ֥י

**"the captivity of Judah who"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **captivity**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[those captured from Judah who]

See: Abstract Nouns

בְּ⁠אַחֲרִ֣ית יוֹמַיָּ֑⁠א

**"at the end of days"**

Daniel is using **at the end of days** as a common expression of the culture to mean a future time. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Daniel 2:28 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_sea4)

חֶלְמָ֨⁠ךְ וְ⁠חֶזְוֵ֥י רֵאשָׁ֛⁠ךְ

**"Your dream and the visions of your head"**

If the connection between this statement and the previous one is not clear, you may want to use a connecting word or phrase to show that **Your dream and the visions of your head** explains what Daniel means when he says that God has made the king know what will happen in the future. Alternate translation: \[Your dream, that is, the visions of your head]

See: Connecting Words and Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:28
* **Associated Articles:** Connecting Words and Phrases (ID: `33335@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:28 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_syau)

וְ⁠חֶזְוֵ֥י רֵאשָׁ֛⁠ךְ

**"and the visions of your head"**

Daniel is using **the visions of your head** as a common expression of the culture to mean the things that Nebuchadnezzar saw in his mind while he was dreaming. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and the images you saw in your mind]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:28

## Daniel 2:29 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_wyi3)

רָזַיָּ֛⁠א

**"mysteries"**

## Daniel 2:30 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_y0jb)

רָזָ֥⁠א דְנָ֖ה גֱּלִ֣י לִ֑⁠י

**"this mystery has been revealed to me"**

The expression **this mystery has been revealed to me** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[God has revealed this mystery to me]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:30
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:30 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_j7fe)

מִן־כָּל־חַיַּיָּ֔⁠א

**"more than all of the living"**

Daniel is using the adjective **living** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[more than anyone else who is alive] or \[more than any other human being]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:30
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:30 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_bk3a)

לְ⁠מַלְכָּ֣⁠א יְהוֹדְע֔וּן

**"they may make the king know"**

The pronoun **they** does not refer to any specific people here. Daniel uses it in an indefinite way to mean that the interpretation would be made known to the king. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[the king may come to know]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:30
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:30 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_vd1i)

וְ⁠רַעְיוֹנֵ֥י לִבְבָ֖⁠ךְ

**"the thoughts of your heart"**

Daniel is speaking of the mind as if it were the **heart**, because the people of this culture associated thinking with the heart. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the thoughts of your mind]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:30
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

וְ⁠רֵוֵ֖⁠הּ דְּחִֽיל

**"and its appearance was terrifying"**

וַ⁠אֲל֨וּ

**"and behold"**

## Daniel 2:31 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_vx42)

צַלְמָ֨⁠א דִּכֵּ֥ן רַ֛ב וְ⁠זִיוֵ֥⁠הּ יַתִּ֖יר

**"This statue {was} great, and its splendor {was} exceeding"**

The expression **was great and its splendor was exceeding** is a passive verbal form. וְ⁠רֵוֵ֖⁠הּ דְּחִֽיל

**"and its appearance was terrifying"**

## Daniel 2:32 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_zx8e)

חֲד֥וֹ⁠הִי וּ⁠דְרָע֖וֹ⁠הִי דִּ֣י כְסַ֑ף מְע֥וֹ⁠הִי וְ⁠יַרְכָתֵ֖⁠הּ דִּ֥י נְחָֽשׁ

**"its chest and arms, of silver; its belly and its thighs, of bronze"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[its chest and arms were of silver; its belly and its thighs were of bronze]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:32
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Daniel 2:33 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_vuh5)

שָׁק֖וֹ⁠הִי דִּ֣י פַרְזֶ֑ל רַגְל֕וֹ⁠הִי מנ⁠הון דִּ֣י פַרְזֶ֔ל ו⁠מנ⁠הון דִּ֥י חֲסַֽף

**"its legs, of iron; its feet, some of them of iron and some of them of clay"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. He means that its legs {were} of iron. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[its legs were of iron; some of its feet were of iron and some of them were of clay]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:33
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:34 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_pyu5)

הִתְגְּזֶ֤רֶת אֶ֨בֶן֙

**"a stone was cut out"**

The expression **a stone was cut out** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[someone cut out a stone]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:34
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:34 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_jkwg)

דִּי־לָ֣א בִ⁠ידַ֔יִן

**"but not by hands"**

Here, **hands** represents the effort or action of human beings. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[but not by human effort]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:34
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:35 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_v1ju)

כְּ⁠ע֣וּר מִן־אִדְּרֵי־קַ֔יִט

**"like the chaff of the threshing floors of summer"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **chaff** is light and easily blown away by the wind, so the crushed pieces of the statue were completely scattered and disappeared. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as light and scattered as the chaff of the threshing floors of summer]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:35
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Threshing Floor (ID: `realia:ThreshingFloor`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

**"and any place was not found for them"**

## Daniel 2:35 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_l39g)

הֲוָ֛ת לְ⁠ט֥וּר רַ֖ב וּ⁠מְלָ֥ת כָּל־אַרְעָֽ⁠א

**"became a great mountain, and it filled all of the earth"**

Daniel is speaking of the kingdom that God will establish as if it were **a great mountain** that **filled all of the earth**. ## Daniel 2:36 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ddfs)

וּ⁠פִשְׁרֵ֖⁠הּ נֵאמַ֥ר קֳדָם־מַלְכָּֽ⁠א

**"And we will tell its interpretation before the king"**

By **we**, Daniel means himself but not the king to whom he is speaking, so use the exclusive form of that word in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

See: Exclusive and Inclusive ‘We’

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:36
* **Associated Articles:** Exclusive and Inclusive ‘We’ (ID: `33271@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:38 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_x4r0)

**"the beast of the field, and the bird of the heavens"**

Daniel is not referring to a specific **beast** or **bird**. He means beasts and birds in general. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[the wild animals, and the birds of the sky]

* **Associated Articles:** Generic Noun Phrases (ID: `33277@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:38 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_xitx)

בִּ⁠ידָ֔⁠ךְ

**"into your hand"**

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:38
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:38 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_kunf)

אַנְתְּה־ה֔וּא רֵאשָׁ֖⁠ה דִּ֥י דַהֲבָֽ⁠א

**"You are the head of gold"**

Daniel is speaking of Nebuchadnezzar as if he were the head of gold on the statue. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[You are like the head of gold]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:38
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:39 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_l39a)

ארע⁠א מִנָּ֑⁠ךְ

**"earthward from you"**

Here, **earthward from you** represents being inferior. since the earth is generally below people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[inferior to you]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:39
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:40 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_mrqr)

תַקִּיפָ֖ה כְּ⁠פַרְזְלָ֑⁠א

**"strong as iron"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **iron** is strong and crushes other things, so **a fourth kingdom** will be strong and will crush the kingdoms before it. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[strong just as iron is strong]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:40
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:42 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ueye)

וְ⁠אֶצְבְּעָת֙ רַגְלַיָּ֔⁠א מנ⁠הון פַּרְזֶ֖ל ו⁠מנ⁠הון חֲסַ֑ף מִן־קְצָ֤ת מַלְכוּתָ⁠א֙ תֶּהֱוֵ֣ה תַקִּיפָ֔ה וּ⁠מִנַּ֖⁠הּ תֶּהֱוֵ֥ה תְבִירָֽה

**"And {as} the toes of the feet, some of them {were} iron and some of them {were} clay, some of the kingdom will be strong and some of it will be brittle"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the toes of the feet** were **some iron and some clay**, so is the kingdom composed of both strong and brittle elements. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[Just as the toes of the feet were partly iron and partly clay, so will some of the kingdom be strong and some of it will be brittle]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:42
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 2:42 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_iwpj)

מנ⁠הון פַּרְזֶ֖ל ו⁠מנ⁠הון חֲסַ֑ף

**"some of them {were} iron and some of them {were} clay"**

In each part of the verse, you could put the **clay** before the **iron** in order to put the new information before the old information. The UST models one way to do that.

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:42
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 2:43 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_ah4r)

הֵֽא־כְ⁠דִ֣י פַרְזְלָ֔⁠א לָ֥א מִתְעָרַ֖ב עִם־חַסְפָּֽ⁠א

**"even as iron does not mix itself with clay"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **iron** does not mix itself with **clay**, so the rulers of this kingdom will not remain united with one another. ## Daniel 2:44 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_mhxx)

**"a kingdom that will not be destroyed to eternities"**

The expression **a kingdom that will not be destroyed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[a kingdom that no one will ever destroy]

**"and the kingdom will not be left to another people"**

## Daniel 2:44 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_sbjz)

וְ⁠הִ֖יא תְּק֥וּם לְ⁠עָלְמַיָּֽ⁠א

**"but it, it will stand to eternities"**

Daniel is stating the pronoun **it** separately, even though the verb translated as **it will stand** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that this kingdom, unlike the kingdoms that came before it, will endure forever. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[but God’s kingdom will stand forever]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 2:44
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel - Chapter 3 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_fqdn)

Daniel 3 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Nebuchadnezzar orders his officials to worship his golden statue (1–7\)
2. Officials report Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego for not worshiping (8–12\)
3. Nebuchadnezzar threatens them, but they refuse (13–18\)
4. Nebuchadnezzar has them thrown into a very hot furnace (19–23\)
5. God sends an angel to rescue them (24–27\)
6. Nebuchadnezzar honors God and promotes them (28–30\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Why did Nebuchadnezzar make a statue covered completely with gold?

When Daniel interprets Nebuchadnezzar’s dream in chapter 2, he explains that the parts of the statue in his dream that were made of different metals represented different empires. Nebuchadnezzar’s Babylonian empire was the head of gold. The dream, which Daniel said God gave Nebuchadnezzar to show him what would happen in the future, indicated that this empire would end and others would succeed it. By building a great statue like the one in his dream but covering it all in gold, Nebuchadnezzar was asserting instead that his empire would never end. Bowing down to the statue was an act of agreeing with him and disagreeing with what God had said in the dream. In many places, the law of Moses already forbade Israelites to bow down to any idols (for example, in the Ten Commandments in Exodus 20:4–5\). But this was a further reason why these Judean young men had to refuse to worship the statue even if they were executed as a result.

### Why does this chapter not describe Daniel refusing to worship the statue?

This chapter only describes Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego refusing to worship Nebuchadnezzar’s golden statue. However, readers should not infer from this that Daniel did worship it. The last verse of the previous chapter, which provides a transition into this chapter, says that Nebuchadnezzar “appointed Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego over the work of the province of Babylon. But Daniel was at the gate of the king.” In other words, Daniel remained at the royal court, while his three friends had responsibilities out in the province of Babylon, and that is where Nebuchadnezzar set up his statue (3:1\). So Daniel was not among the provincial officials who were summoned to the dedication of the statue. His actions in the rest of the book (for example, in chapter 6, where he continues to pray to God even though he would be executed for doing this) show that if he had been summoned, he too would have refused to worship the statue.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:0


## Daniel 3:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_kgd4)

אַמִּ֣ין שִׁתִּ֔ין פְּתָיֵ֖⁠הּ אַמִּ֣ין שִׁ֑ת

**"was} sixty cubits. Its width {was} six cubits"**

These **cubits** were a unit of measurement corresponding to the distance between an average person’s elbow and middle fingertip, or about 46 centimeters or 18 inches. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could express these measurements in modern units, either in the text or a footnote. Alternate translation: \[about 27 meters. Its width {was} about 2\.7 meters]

See: Biblical Distance

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Biblical Distance (ID: `33369@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 3:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_jn5a)

קַרְנָ⁠א֩ מַשְׁר֨וֹקִיתָ֜⁠א קיתרס שַׂבְּכָא֙ פְּסַנְטֵרִ֔ין

**"the horn, pipe, lyre, trigon, harp"**

Some ancient manuscripts do not include the term “bagpipe” at the end of the list of instruments here. The ULT follows that reading. Other ancient manuscripts include the term, as in verses 5, 10, and 15\. If a translation of the Bible exists in your region, you may wish to use the reading that it uses. If a translation of the Bible does not exist in your region, you may wish to use the reading of the ULT.

See: Textual Variants

## Daniel 3:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_sc6w)

עֲנוֹ֙ וְ⁠אָ֣מְרִ֔ין

**"They answered and were saying"**

The Chaldeans had not been asked a question, but Daniel uses the expression **They answered and were saying** to introduce their speech. Consider natural ways of introducing direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:9
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 3:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_p9n9)

לְ⁠עָלְמִ֥ין חֱיִֽי

**"live to eternities"**

The Chaldeans are using **live to eternities** as a common expression of the culture to mean “may you live for a very long time.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[may you live many years]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:9
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 3:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_nfd9)

גֻּבְרִ֣ין יְהוּדָאיִ֗ן

**"men, Jews"**

The Chaldeans are identifying Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego as Jews. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[certain Jews]

See: Introduction of New and Old Participants

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:12
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of New and Old Participants (ID: `33421@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)

## Daniel 3:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_oe2m)

גֻּבְרַיָּ֣⁠א אִלֵּ֗ךְ לָא־שָׂ֨מֽוּ עלי⁠ך \& טְעֵ֔ם

**"These men do not set regard on you"**

The Chaldeans are using **do not set regard on you** as a common expression of the culture to mean “do not respect you” or “do not obey you.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[These men do not respect you] or \[These men do not obey you]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:12
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)

## Daniel 3:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_oltc)

בִּ⁠רְגַ֣ז וַ⁠חֲמָ֔ה

**"in rage and heat"**

The terms **rage** and **heat** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[in furious anger]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:13
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xmcu)

תִּפְּל֣וּן וְ⁠תִסְגְּדוּן֮ לְ⁠צַלְמָ֣⁠א דִֽי־עַבְדֵת֒ וְ⁠הֵן֙ לָ֣א תִסְגְּד֔וּן

**"you fall down and worship the statue that I have made; but if you do not worship"**

Nebuchadnezzar is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[you fall down and worship the statue that I have made, then I will not punish you for not doing this before; but if you do not worship]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:15
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bagpipe (ID: `realia:Bagpipe`); Lute (ID: `realia:Lute`); Lyre (ID: `realia:Lyre`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Flute (ID: `realia:Flute`)

## Daniel 3:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_rebv)

וּ⁠מַן־ה֣וּא אֱלָ֔הּ דֵּ֥י יְשֵֽׁיזְבִנְ⁠כ֖וֹן מִן־יְדָֽ⁠י

**"And who is the god who will deliver you from my hand"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the question form to assert that no god will be able to rescue Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego from his power. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[And there is no god who will deliver you from my hand!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:15
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bagpipe (ID: `realia:Bagpipe`); Lute (ID: `realia:Lute`); Lyre (ID: `realia:Lyre`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Flute (ID: `realia:Flute`)

## Daniel 3:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_arpw)

יְדָֽ⁠י

**"my hand"**

Here, **my hand** represents Nebuchadnezzar’s power to punish them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[my control]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:15
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bagpipe (ID: `realia:Bagpipe`); Lute (ID: `realia:Lute`); Lyre (ID: `realia:Lyre`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Flute (ID: `realia:Flute`)

## Daniel 3:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_a5tq)

הֵ֣ן אִיתַ֗י

**"If it is"**

Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego are using **If it is** as a common expression of the culture to mean “if that happens,” referring to their being thrown into the furnace. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[If we are thrown into the furnace] or \[If that is what happens]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:17
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_r9q3)

יְדָ֥⁠ךְ

**"your hand"**

Here, **your hand** represents the king’s power. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[your control]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:17
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_yt0p)

וְ⁠הֵ֣ן לָ֔א

**"But if not"**

Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego are leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[But if he does not deliver us]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:18
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:18 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_prhj)

יְדִ֥יעַ לֶהֱוֵא־לָ֖⁠ךְ

**"may it be known to you"**

The expression **may it be known to you** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[you should know]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:18
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_rfuc)

הִתְמְלִ֣י חֱמָ֗א

**"was filled {with} heat"**

Daniel is using **was filled {with} heat** as a common expression of the culture to mean “became furious.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[became furious]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:19
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_xoj3)

חַ֨ד־שִׁבְעָ֔ה עַ֛ל דִּ֥י חֲזֵ֖ה לְ⁠מֵזְיֵֽ⁠הּ

**"one seven more than had been seen to heat it"**

Daniel is using **one seven more** as a common expression of the culture to mean “seven times hotter” than it was usually heated. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[seven times hotter]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:19
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:19 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_tvx8)

דִּ֥י חֲזֵ֖ה לְ⁠מֵזְיֵֽ⁠הּ

**"than had been seen to heat it"**

Daniel is using the phrase **than had been seen to heat it** as a common expression of the culture to mean “more than it had ever been heated before.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[than it had ever been heated before]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:19
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:19 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_ynjf)

דִּ֥י חֲזֵ֖ה לְ⁠מֵזְיֵֽ⁠הּ

**"than had been seen to heat it"**

The expression **had been seen** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[than anyone had seen anyone heat it]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:19
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:19 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_yy6i)

חַ֨ד־שִׁבְעָ֔ה עַ֛ל דִּ֥י חֲזֵ֖ה לְ⁠מֵזְיֵֽ⁠הּ

**"one seven more than had been seen to heat it"**

Here, **seven** might not be an exact number. It may represent completion. Alternate translation: \[as many times hotter as possible, hotter than it had ever been heated before]

See: Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:19
* **Associated Articles:** Numbers (ID: `33392@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:20 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_sera)

וּ⁠לְ⁠גֻבְרִ֤ין גִּבָּֽרֵי־חַ֨יִל֙ \& אֲמַר֙

**"And he told men, men of valor"**

The author is using the expression **men, men of valor** to introduce these new participants in the story. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[certain men of valor] or \[certain strong soldiers]

See: Introduction of New and Old Participants

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:20
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of New and Old Participants (ID: `33421@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:21 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ednd)

פטישי⁠הון

**"their tunics"**

The term that the ULT translates as **tunics** refers to some kind of inner garment that people of this culture wore under their **cloaks**. Some interpreters understand it to mean “trousers.” However, since the exact meaning is uncertain, you may wish to use a general phrase in your translation. Alternate translation: \[their inner garments]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:21
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:22 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_aot3)

כָּ⁠ל־קֳבֵ֣ל דְּנָ֗ה

**"All before this"**

The author is using **All before this** as a common expression of the culture to mean “because of this” or “on account of this.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Because of this] or \[For this reason]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:22
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:25 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ah4e)

הָֽא

**"Behold"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the term **Behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[Look]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:25


## Daniel 3:25 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_poei)

וְ⁠רֵוֵ⁠הּ֙ דִּ֣י רביעי⁠א

**"And the appearance of the fourth"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **appearance**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[And the fourth one]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:25
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:25 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_hp1o)

לְ⁠בַר־אֱלָהִֽין

**"a son of the gods"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using **a son of the gods** as a common expression of the culture to mean “a god.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[a god]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:25
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:26 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xx6w)

עַבְד֛וֹ⁠הִי דִּֽי־אֱלָהָ֥⁠א עלי⁠א

**"his servants of the Most High God"**

It might seem that saying **his** in addition to the possessive phrase with **of** would be to express extra information in a way that would not be natural in your language. If so, you can shorten this expression. Alternate translation: \[servants of the Most High God]

See: Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:26
* **Associated Articles:** Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit (ID: `33274@UWTranslationNotes`)
