## Daniel 3:27 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_dygr)

לָֽא־שְׁלֵ֨ט נוּרָ֜⁠א בְּ⁠גֶשְׁמְ⁠ה֗וֹן

**"the fire had not ruled over their bodies"**

Daniel is speaking of **the fire** as if it were a living thing that could rule over the men’s bodies. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the fire had not damaged their bodies]

See: Personification

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:27
* **Associated Articles:** Personification (ID: `33302@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 3:28 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_yr0i)

בְּרִ֤יךְ אֱלָהֲ⁠הוֹן֙ דִּֽי־שַׁדְרַ֤ךְ מֵישַׁךְ֙ וַ⁠עֲבֵ֣ד נְג֔וֹ

**"Blessed {be} the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego"**

Nebuchadnezzar is praising God when he says **Blessed {be} the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego**. In your translation, use a form that people would recognize as an expression of praise or blessing in your language. You might express this as a declaration of praise, if that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[May the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego be praised]

See: Blessings

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:28
* **Associated Articles:** Blessings (ID: `33371@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`)

## Daniel 3:28 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_h1uh)

וִ⁠יהַ֣בוּ גשמי⁠הון

**"and gave up their bodies"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using **gave up their bodies** as a common expression of the culture to mean “were willing to die.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and were willing to die]

## Daniel 3:29 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_mznu)

וּ⁠מִ⁠נִּי֮ שִׂ֣ים טְעֵם֒

**"And from me a decree is set"**

The expression **And from me a decree is set** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And I am setting forth this decree]

See: Active or Passive

## Daniel 3:29 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_vc8e)

שלה

**"anything"**

The phrase **anything** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text as we have it today. There is, however, a correction, “anything wrong,” in the margin of the text that Hebrew scribes have recommended. You may want to follow this correction in your translation, or you may want to follow the major translation in your area.

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Daniel 3:29 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_g9zl)

הַדָּמִ֣ין יִתְעֲבֵ֔ד וּ⁠בַיְתֵ֖⁠הּ נְוָלִ֣י יִשְׁתַּוֵּ֑ה

**"will be made {into} limbs and its house be made a rubbish heap"**

The expressions **will be made {into} limbs** and **its house be made a rubbish heap** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[I will have my soldiers cut them into limbs and make their house a rubbish heap]

## Daniel 3:29 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_zqyi)

הַדָּמִ֣ין יִתְעֲבֵ֔ד

**"will be made {into} limbs"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using **will be made {into} limbs** as a common expression of the culture to mean “be cut into pieces.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[will be cut into pieces]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 3:29 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_vnfs)

כָּ⁠ל־קֳבֵ֗ל דִּ֣י

**"all before that"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using **all before that** as a common expression of the culture to mean “because.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[seeing that] or \[because]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:29
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Azariah (ID: `person:Azariah.20`); Hananiah (ID: `person:Hananiah.15`); Mishael (ID: `person:Mishael.3`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Daniel 3:29 (#6) (id: en_tn_DAN_e4co)

וּ⁠בַיְתֵ֖⁠הּ נְוָלִ֣י יִשְׁתַּוֵּ֑ה

**"and its house be made a rubbish heap"**

Nebuchadnezzar is speaking as if the offender’s house were **a rubbish heap**. He means that it will be completely destroyed and reduced to ruins. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and its house be completely destroyed]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 3:29
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel - Chapter 4 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_twwh)

Daniel 4 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter consists of a letter that King Nebuchadnezzar wrote to all the subjects in his empire. In this letter, he relates an experience he had that taught him that God, whom he calls “the Most High” and “the king of the heavens,” determines what people will reign over earthly kingdoms and for how long.

1. The greeting at the start of the letter (1–3\)
2. Nebuchadnezzar has a dream and asks Daniel to interpret it (4–18\)
3. Daniel explains the meaning of the dream (19–27\)
4. What the dream depicted happens to Nebuchadnezzar (28–36\)
5. The closing of the letter (37\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 4:3 and 4:34–35\.

### The tree as the king

The great tree of the dream represents Nebuchadnezzar himself (verse 22\); keep the imagery while letting the interpretation identify him.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:0


## Daniel 4:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_zoah)

עַֽמְמַיָּ֞⁠א אֻמַיָּ֧⁠א וְ⁠לִשָּׁנַיָּ֛⁠א

**"the peoples, nations, and languages"**

The terms **peoples**, **nations**, and **languages** mean similar things. Nebuchadnezzar is using the three terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[the many different people groups]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 4:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_alo5)

וְ⁠לִשָּׁנַיָּ֛⁠א

**"and languages"**

Here, **languages** represents the people who speak them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and language groups]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 4:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_ja8y)

שְׁלָמְ⁠כ֥וֹן יִשְׂגֵּֽא

**"May your peace abound"**

This is a conventional greeting at the start of a letter that expresses the hope that all is well with the recipients. (The term **peace** has the sense of “welfare” here.) Use the equivalent greeting from your culture. The UST models one way to do that.

See: Politeness

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Politeness (ID: `33423@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 4:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_jtzu)

אָת֨וֹ⁠הִי֙ כְּ⁠מָ֣ה רַבְרְבִ֔ין וְ⁠תִמְה֖וֹ⁠הִי כְּ⁠מָ֣ה תַקִּיפִ֑ין

**"How great {are} his signs, and how mighty {are} his wonders"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[How very great and mighty are his miraculous signs!]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_hm3r)

מַלְכוּתֵ⁠הּ֙ מַלְכ֣וּת עָלַ֔ם

**"His kingdom {is} a kingdom of eternity"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **kingdom**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[He rules as king forever]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_eywn)

מַלְכוּתֵ⁠הּ֙ מַלְכ֣וּת עָלַ֔ם וְ⁠שָׁלְטָנֵ֖⁠הּ עִם־דָּ֥ר וְ⁠דָֽר

**"His kingdom {is} a kingdom of eternity, and his dominion {is} with generation and generation"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Another approach would be to connect the phrases with a word that shows that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. See how you as a team have decided to translate the parallel phrases in poetry, and follow that approach here and throughout this chapter. Alternate translation: \[His kingdom is an everlasting kingdom, indeed, he rules over people from generation to generation]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_y5pe)

עִם־דָּ֥ר וְ⁠דָֽר

**"is} with generation and generation"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the phrase **with generation and generation** as a common expression of the culture to mean “for all generations” or “forever.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[continuing throughout all generations]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"I, Nebuchadnezzar, was being secure in my house and prosperous in my palace"**

## Daniel 4:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_h4tq)

חֵ֥לֶם חֲזֵ֖ית וִֽ⁠ידַחֲלִנַּ֑⁠נִי וְ⁠הַרְהֹרִין֙ עַֽל־מִשְׁכְּבִ֔⁠י וְ⁠חֶזְוֵ֥י רֵאשִׁ֖⁠י יְבַהֲלֻנַּֽ⁠נִי

**"I saw a dream and it frightened me. And the thoughts upon my bed and the visions of my head alarmed me"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Another approach would be to connect the phrases with a word that shows that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. See how you as a team have decided to translate the parallel phrases in poetry, and follow that approach here and throughout this chapter. Alternate translation: \[I saw a dream that terrified and alarmed me as I lay on my bed]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_wt2h)

**"and the visions of my head"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the phrase **the visions of my head** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the things that he saw while he was dreaming.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and the things I saw in my dream]

## Daniel 4:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_n3br)

חָזֵ֣ה הֲוֵ֔ית וַ⁠אֲל֥וּ אִילָ֛ן בְּ⁠ג֥וֹא אַרְעָ֖⁠א

**"I was looking, and behold, a tree in the midst of the earth"**

Nebuchadnezzar is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. He means that he saw a tree in his vision. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[I was looking, and listen, I saw a tree in the midst of the earth]

See: Ellipsis

## Daniel 4:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_stoy)

וְ⁠רוּמֵ⁠הּ֙ יִמְטֵ֣א לִ⁠שְׁמַיָּ֔⁠א וַ⁠חֲזוֹתֵ֖⁠הּ לְ⁠ס֥וֹף כָּל־אַרְעָֽ⁠א

**"and its height reached to the heavens, and its sight, to the end of all of the earth"**

## Daniel 4:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_seyg)

תְּנֻ֤ד חֵֽיוְתָ⁠א֙ מִן־תַּחְתּ֔וֹ⁠הִי וְ⁠צִפְּרַיָּ֖⁠א מִן־עַנְפֽוֹ⁠הִי

**"Let the beast flee from beneath it, and the birds, from its branches"**

Alternate translation: \[The beast must flee from beneath it, and the birds, from its branches]

See: Third\-Person Imperatives

**"and the birds, from its branches"**

## Daniel 4:14 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_b6n8)

גֹּ֤דּוּ אִֽילָנָ⁠א֙ וְ⁠קַצִּ֣צוּ עַנְפ֔וֹ⁠הִי אַתַּ֥רוּ עָפְיֵ֖⁠הּ וּ⁠בַדַּ֣רוּ אִנְבֵּ֑⁠הּ תְּנֻ֤ד חֵֽיוְתָ⁠א֙ מִן־תַּחְתּ֔וֹ⁠הִי וְ⁠צִפְּרַיָּ֖⁠א מִן־עַנְפֽוֹ⁠הִי

**"Cut down the tree and cut off its branches, shake off its foliage, and scatter its fruit. Let the beast flee from beneath it, and the birds, from its branches"**

The implied word “you” in these commands is plural because the watcher is addressing the other heavenly beings who carry out his commands, so use the plural form in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

See: Forms of ‘You’ — Singular

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:14
* **Associated Articles:** Forms of ‘You’ — Singular (ID: `33321@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_qbgy)

שְׁבֻ֔קוּ

**"leave"**

The word “you” is implied in the command **leave** and is plural here because the watcher is speaking to the other heavenly beings, so use the plural form in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

See: Forms of ‘You’ — Singular

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:15
* **Associated Articles:** Forms of ‘You’ — Singular (ID: `33321@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Daniel 4:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_cz7x)

וּ⁠בֶֽ⁠אֱסוּר֙ דִּֽי־פַרְזֶ֣ל וּ⁠נְחָ֔שׁ

**"and with a band of iron and bronze"**

The purpose of this **band** was to prevent the heat of the sun from splitting the tree and killing it completely. If the stump remained intact, it might send out new shoots. If your readers would not be familiar with the practice of banding a tree stump, you could explain it in the text of your translation or in a footnote.

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:15
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Daniel 4:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_n7e9)

בְּ⁠דִתְאָ֖⁠א \& בַּ⁠עֲשַׂ֥ב

**"in the grass \& in the grass of"**

The second instance of “grass” refers to grass in general. The first instance of “grass” refers to new grass or tender grass, that is, grass that has just started growing. If your language has its own term for this, it would be appropriate to use it here in your translation, here and in verse 23\.

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:15
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Daniel 4:15 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_eh2l)

וּ⁠בְ⁠טַ֤ל שְׁמַיָּ⁠א֙ יִצְטַבַּ֔ע

**"And let it be wet with the dew of the heavens"**

The expression **be wet** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And let the dew of the heavens make it wet]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:15
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Daniel 4:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_kyyu)

לִבְבֵ⁠הּ֙ מִן־אנוש⁠א יְשַׁנּ֔וֹן וּ⁠לְבַ֥ב חֵיוָ֖ה יִתְיְהִ֣ב לֵ֑⁠הּ וְ⁠שִׁבְעָ֥ה עִדָּנִ֖ין יַחְלְפ֥וּן עֲלֽוֹ⁠הִי

**"Let his heart be changed from a man, and let the heart of a beast be given to him, and let seven times pass over him"**

The watcher is saying what he wants to happen to the man represented by the tree. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and “may” and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants someone to do. Alternate translation: \[I desire that his heart be changed from a man and that the heart of a beast be given to him and that seven times pass over him] or \[His heart should be changed from a man’s, and the heart of a beast should be given to him, and seven years should pass over him]

See: Third\-Person Imperatives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:16


## Daniel 4:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_pney)

לִבְבֵ⁠הּ֙ מִן־אנוש⁠א יְשַׁנּ֔וֹן וּ⁠לְבַ֥ב חֵיוָ֖ה יִתְיְהִ֣ב לֵ֑⁠הּ

**"Let his heart be changed from a man, and let the heart of a beast be given to him"**

The expressions **be changed** and **be given** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[Let them change his heart from a man, and let them give the heart of a beast to him]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:16
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:16 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_p6ku)

לִבְבֵ⁠הּ֙ מִן־אנוש⁠א יְשַׁנּ֔וֹן

**"Let his heart be changed from a man"**

The pronoun **his** refers to the man whom the tree represents. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Let the heart of the man whom the tree represents be changed from a man]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:16
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:16 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_glh3)

לִבְבֵ⁠הּ֙ מִן־אנוש⁠א יְשַׁנּ֔וֹן

**"Let his heart be changed from a man"**

Here the watcher is speaking as if the man’s mind were his **heart**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Let his mind be changed from a man]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:16
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:16 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_vnd8)

וְ⁠שִׁבְעָ֥ה עִדָּנִ֖ין יַחְלְפ֥וּן עֲלֽוֹ⁠הִי

**"and let seven times pass over him"**

The implication is that **seven times** refers to a fixed period of time, either seven years or seven seasons. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[and let seven fixed periods of time pass over him]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:16
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_br95)

בִּ⁠גְזֵרַ֤ת עִירִין֙ פִּתְגָמָ֔⁠א וּ⁠מֵאמַ֥ר קַדִּישִׁ֖ין שְׁאֵֽלְתָ֑⁠א

**"The matter {is} by a decree of the watchers, and the decision {is} from a command of the holy ones"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[The watchers and holy ones have decided this together]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:17
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 4:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_pc9m)

חַ֠יַּיָּ⁠א

**"the living"**

The speaker for **the watchers** is using the adjective **the living** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[people who are alive]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:17
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 4:17 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_ynit)

וּ⁠שְׁפַ֥ל אֲנָשִׁ֖ים יְקִ֥ים עלי⁠ה

**"and he sets up over it the lowest of men"**

The watcher is using the adjective **the lowest** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[and he sets up over it a man who is of the lowest status]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:17
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 4:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_tkxy)

דִּ֛י רֽוּחַ־אֱלָהִ֥ין קַדִּישִׁ֖ין בָּֽ⁠ךְ

**"because the spirit of the holy gods {is} in you"**

The word translated as **because** indicates that what follows is a reason for what came before. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that what follows is a reason for what came before. Alternate translation: \[since the spirit of the holy gods is in you]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:18
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 4:18 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_a77s)

כָּ⁠ל־קֳבֵל֙ דִּ֣י ׀ כָּל־חַכִּימֵ֣י מַלְכוּתִ֗⁠י

**"all before that all of the wise men of my kingdom"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using \*\*all before that \*\* as a common expression of the culture to mean “since.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[since all of the wise men of my kingdom]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:18
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 4:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_wkvc)

חֶלְמָ֥⁠א ל⁠שנאי⁠ך וּ⁠פִשְׁרֵ֥⁠הּ ל⁠ערי⁠ך

**"may} the dream {be} for the ones hating you and its interpretation, for your enemies"**

Daniel is using the expression **for the ones hating you and its interpretation, for your enemies** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I wish that what the dream predicts would happen to your enemies rather than to you.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I wish that what the dream and its interpretation predict would happen to those who hate you, your enemies, rather than to you]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:19
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 4:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_em22)

מרא⁠י חֶלְמָ֥⁠א ל⁠שנאי⁠ך וּ⁠פִשְׁרֵ֥⁠הּ ל⁠ערי⁠ך

**"My lord, {may} the dream {be} for the ones hating you and its interpretation, for your enemies"**

Belteshazzar is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[My lord, may the dream be for the ones hating you and may its interpretation be for your enemies]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:19
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 4:22 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_tudi)

אנתה־ה֣וּא מַלְכָּ֔⁠א

**"it {is} you, O king"**

Here Daniel is speaking as if the king were the tree that Nebuchadnezzar saw in his dream. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[that tree is a symbol of you, O king]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:22
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:22 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ck2l)

וּ⁠רְבוּתָ֤⁠ךְ רְבָת֙

**"And your greatness has become great"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **greatness**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[And how great you are has increased]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:22
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:22 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_on10)

וְ⁠שָׁלְטָנָ֖⁠ךְ לְ⁠ס֥וֹף אַרְעָֽ⁠א

**"and your dominion, to the end of the earth"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and your dominion has reached to the end of the earth]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:22
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:25 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xa3o)

וְ⁠לָ֣⁠ךְ טָֽרְדִ֣ין מִן־אֲנָשָׁ֡⁠א \& וְ⁠עִשְׂבָּ֥⁠א כְ⁠תוֹרִ֣ין ׀ לָ֣⁠ךְ יְטַֽעֲמ֗וּן וּ⁠מִ⁠טַּ֤ל שְׁמַיָּ⁠א֙ לָ֣⁠ךְ מְצַבְּעִ֔ין

**"that they will be driving you from man \& And they will feed you grass like oxen, and they will be wetting you with the dew of the heavens"**

In each of these instances, the pronoun **they** does not refer to specific people. Daniel is using it to describe what will happen to the king. In some languages, it may be natural to use passive verbal forms to express this meaning. Alternate translation: \[you will be driven from men … And you will be fed grass like oxen, and you will be wetted with the dew of the heavens]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:25
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 4:25 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_u930)

וְ⁠עִשְׂבָּ֥⁠א כְ⁠תוֹרִ֣ין ׀ לָ֣⁠ךְ יְטַֽעֲמ֗וּן

**"And they will feed you grass like oxen"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **oxen** eat **grass**, so the king will be made to eat grass. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[And they will make you eat grass, just as oxen eat grass]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:25
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 4:25 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_hbqz)

וְ⁠שִׁבְעָ֥ה עִדָּנִ֖ין יַחְלְפ֣וּן עֲלָ֑⁠יךְ

**"And seven times will pass over you"**

Daniel is using **And seven times will pass over you** as a common expression of the culture to mean “seven years will pass for you.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Seven years will go by for you] or \[This will continue for seven years]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:25
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 4:26 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ttpt)

וְ⁠דִ֣י אֲמַ֗רוּ לְ⁠מִשְׁבַּ֞ק עִקַּ֤ר שָׁרְשׁ֨וֹ⁠הִי֙ דִּ֣י אִֽילָנָ֔⁠א מַלְכוּתָ֖⁠ךְ לָ֣⁠ךְ קַיָּמָ֑ה

**"And that they said to leave the stump of the roots of the tree, your kingdom {will be} enduring for you"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about the king’s kingdom enduring before the information about leaving the stump of the roots of the tree. Alternate translation: \[And your kingdom will be an enduring kingdom for you; that is what they meant when they said to leave the stump of the roots of the tree]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:26
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:26 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_j73z)

שַׁלִּטִ֖ן שְׁמַיָּֽ⁠א

**"the heavens {are} ruler"**

Here, **the heavens** represents “God.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[God is ruler]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:26
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:27 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_hkvw)

ו⁠חטי⁠ך בְּ⁠צִדְקָ֣ה פְרֻ֔ק וַ⁠עֲוָיָתָ֖⁠ךְ

**"and may you break off your sin in righteousness and your iniquities"**

Here Daniel is speaking as if the king could **break off** his sin the way a person breaks off a branch from a tree. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and may you cease from your sin by doing what is right and from your iniquities]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:27
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:27 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ybnr)

הֵ֛ן

**"if"**

Here, the term **if** marks a prolonging of your prosperity as the possible result of Nebuchadnezzar breaking off his sin to practice righteousness and his iniquity by showing mercy to the poor. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that Daniel is suggesting that this could be a result. Alternate translation: \[so that]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:27
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:27 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_kvqp)

בְּ⁠צִדְקָ֣ה

**"in righteousness"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **righteousness**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[by doing what is right]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:27
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:27 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_rl0m)

עֲנָ֑יִן

**"the poor"**

Daniel is using the adjective **the poor** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[those who are poor]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:27
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 4:28 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xvms)

כֹּ֣לָּ⁠א מְּטָ֔א עַל־נְבוּכַדְנֶצַּ֖ר מַלְכָּֽ⁠א

**"The whole came upon King Nebuchadnezzar"**

Nebuchadnezzar is the one telling this account, but he speaks of himself in the third person as **King Nebuchadnezzar**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[Everything from the dream came upon me, King Nebuchadnezzar]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:28
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 4:29 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_g7yd)

לִ⁠קְצָ֥ת יַרְחִ֖ין תְּרֵֽי־עֲשַׂ֑ר

**"At the end of 12 months"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the phrase translated as **At the end of 12 months** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:29
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`)

## Daniel 4:30 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_tvf7)

הֲ⁠לָ֥א דָא־הִ֖יא בָּבֶ֣ל רַבְּתָ֑⁠א דִּֽי־אֲנָ֤ה בֱנַיְתַ⁠הּ֙ לְ⁠בֵ֣ית מַלְכ֔וּ בִּ⁠תְקַ֥ף חִסְנִ֖⁠י וְ⁠לִ⁠יקָ֥ר הַדְרִֽ⁠י

**"Is} this not the great Babylon, which I, I have built it for the house of the kingdom by the might of my power and for the glory of my majesty"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the question form to boast about what he has accomplished. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[This is indeed the great Babylon, which I myself have built for the house of the kingdom by the might of my power and for the glory of my majesty!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:30
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Daniel 4:30 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_tbsq)

דִּֽי־אֲנָ֤ה בֱנַיְתַ⁠הּ֙

**"which I, I have built it"**

It might seem that the expression **which I, I have built it** contains extra information that would not be natural to express in your language. If so, you could shorten it. Alternate translation: \[which I have built]

See: Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:30
* **Associated Articles:** Making Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information Explicit (ID: `33274@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Daniel 4:30 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_huk3)

אֲנָ֤ה בֱנַיְתַ⁠הּ֙

**"I, I have built it"**

Nebuchadnezzar is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **have built** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that he himself is the one who built this great city, showing his pride in his own achievement. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[which I myself have built it]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:30
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Daniel 4:31 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ehwk)

ע֗וֹד מִלְּתָ⁠א֙ בְּ⁠פֻ֣ם מַלְכָּ֔⁠א

**"The word {was} still in the mouth of the king"**

Nebuchadnezzar is using the expression **still in the mouth of the king** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the king was still speaking.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The king was still speaking these words]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:31
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** A Disembodied Voice (ID: `deity:Voice`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`)

## Daniel 4:33 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_kk80)

וּ⁠מִן־אֲנָשָׁ֣⁠א טְרִ֔יד וְ⁠עִשְׂבָּ֤⁠א כְ⁠תוֹרִין֙ יֵאכֻ֔ל וּ⁠מִ⁠טַּ֥ל שְׁמַיָּ֖⁠א גִּשְׁמֵ֣⁠הּ יִצְטַבַּ֑ע

**"And he was driven from man, and he ate grass like oxen, and his body was wet with the dew of the heavens"**

The expressions **was driven** and **was wet** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And people drove him away from man, and he ate grass like oxen, and the dew of the heavens made his body wet]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 4:33
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Vulture (ID: `fauna:Vulture`)

## Daniel 4:33 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ao9n)

שַׂעְרֵ֛⁠הּ כְּ⁠נִשְׁרִ֥ין רְבָ֖ה וְ⁠טִפְר֥וֹ⁠הִי כְ⁠צִפְּרִֽין

**"his hair had become great like eagles and his nails, like birds"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **eagles** have long feathers and **birds** have long claws, so the king’s hair and nails grew very long. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[his hair had grown very long, as long as the feathers of eagles, and his nails had grown very long, as long as the claws of birds]

See: Simile

## Daniel - Chapter 5 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_um6p)

Daniel 5 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. At a feast that Belshazzar gives, a hand writes something on the palace wall (1–6\)
2. No one else can read the writing, but the queen mother recommends Daniel (7–12\)
3. Daniel reads and interprets the writing (13–28\)
4. Belshazzar rewards Daniel, but he is killed that night by the invading Persians (29–30\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Why did Belshazzar want to drink from vessels from the Jerusalem temple?

Belshazzar and his guests used vessels from the Jerusalem temple to drink toasts in honor of the gods their idols represented. This was most likely a deliberate act repudiating the God of that temple in favor of traditional Babylonian gods. Daniel reminds Belshazzar that he knew all about how his grandfather Nebuchadnezzar had come to honor and worship that God as the true God. But many interpreters suggest that, with the Babylonians now under dire threat from the Persians, Belshazzar believed that if he returned to his traditional gods, they would rescue him and his kingdom. In this culture, when one kingdom conquered another, it would take the idols of the conquered kingdom and put them in the temple of one of its own gods as trophies of victory. There were no idols representing Yahweh, so the Babylonians took the vessels from his temple instead. It appears that Nebuchadnezzar had kept these in a temple and not used them for common purposes. Belshazzar, by using them at a banquet to drink toasts to false gods, was defiling them. That is why he came under such serious and immediate judgment.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Mene, Mene, Tekel, and Parsin

No one could read what the writing on the wall said, so it is probable that it was written in some language and script other than the Aramaic that was spoken in Babylon. God enabled Daniel to understand what the writing was saying, and he translated it into Aramaic words. The meaning of these words is evident from what Daniel says about them: “mene” means to “number,” “tekel” means to “weigh,” and “peres” means to “divide.” (The term “mene” is probably repeated for emphasis, to show the certainty of God’s judgment against Belshazzar. “Parsin” in verse 25 is the plural of “peres” in verse 28\.) The ULT and UST texts write these Aramaic words with English letters, spelling them the way they sound in English. Translators are encouraged to write these words with the letters of their own alphabets and to spell them the way they sound in their own languages.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:0

## Daniel 5:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_vjdb)

אֲלַ֑ף

**"a thousand"**

Daniel is using the adjective **thousand** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[a thousand of them]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 5:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_sm6e)

בֵּלְשַׁאצַּ֣ר מַלְכָּ֗⁠א עֲבַד֙ לְחֶ֣ם רַ֔ב לְ⁠רַבְרְבָנ֖וֹ⁠הִי אֲלַ֑ף

**"King Belshazzar made a great feast for his nobles, a thousand"**

Belshazzar is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. He means that he made a feast for a thousand of his nobles. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[King Belshazzar made a great feast for a thousand of his nobles]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`)

## Daniel 5:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_qtne)

בִּ⁠טְעֵ֣ם חַמְרָ֗⁠א

**"in the taste of the wine"**

Daniel is using the phrase **in the taste of the wine** as a common expression of the culture to mean that Belshazzar was under the influence of the wine that he had been drinking. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Belshazzar became drunk and said] or \[while under the influence of the wine]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Daniel 5:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_fxkc)

בֵּלְשַׁאצַּ֞ר אֲמַ֣ר ׀ בִּ⁠טְעֵ֣ם חַמְרָ֗⁠א

**"Belshazzar said in the taste of the wine"**

Elsewhere in Daniel and Ezra, in the Aramaic portions of Scripture, the term that the ULT translates here as **taste** means “command” or “decree.” While many interpreters favor “taste” here, the sense of “command” would also suit the context and have the same basic meaning. Alternate translation: \[Belshazzar said by command of the wine]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 5:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_aq87)

וְ⁠יִשְׁתּ֣וֹן בְּ⁠ה֗וֹן מַלְכָּ⁠א֙ וְ⁠רַבְרְבָנ֔וֹ⁠הִי שֵׁגְלָתֵ֖⁠הּ וּ⁠לְחֵנָתֵֽ⁠הּ

**"so that the king and his nobles, his wives and his concubines might drink from them"**

Drinking from these **vessels** was a symbolic action by which the king mocked the God whose temple was in Jerusalem. Verse 4 makes clear that the Belshazzar wanted to honor his own idols rather than God, whom his grandfather Nebuchadnezzar had learned to fear and honor. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text or in a footnote.

See: Symbolic Action

**"and may your splendors not be changed"**

The expression **may your splendors not be changed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and do not let your face lose its color]

The queen is using the possessive form to describe a **spirit** that is characterized by coming from **the holy gods**. **"light and understanding and wisdom"**

וְ⁠חָכְמָ֥ה כְּ⁠חָכְמַת־אֱלָהִ֖ין

**"and wisdom like the wisdom of the gods"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the gods** were thought to have perfect wisdom, so this man had extraordinary **wisdom**. ## Daniel 5:11 (#6) (id: en_tn_DAN_g558)

וּ⁠מַלְכָּ֤⁠א נְבֻֽכַדְנֶצַּר֙ אֲב֔⁠וּךְ \& אֲב֥⁠וּךְ מַלְכָּֽ⁠א

**"And King Nebuchadnezzar, your father, your father the king"**

The queen is repeating the information that Nebuchadnezzar was an illustrious predecessor of Belshazzar in order to emphasize the qualifications of Daniel. If your language can repeat information for emphasis, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[And your grandfather, King Nebuchadnezzar, that great emperor]

See: Reduplication

כָּ⁠ל־קֳבֵ֡ל דִּ֣י

**"Then Daniel was brought in"**

## Daniel 5:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_zs2m)

בְּנֵ֤י גָלוּתָ⁠א֙ דִּ֣י יְה֔וּד דִּ֥י

**"the sons of the captivity of Judah that"**

The king is using the phrase **the sons of the captivity of Judah** as a common expression of the culture to refer to the people who had been taken away from Judah as captives. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the Judean captives whom]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:13
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 5:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_x9kq)

אנתה־ה֤וּא דָנִיֵּאל֙ דִּֽי־מִן־בְּנֵ֤י גָלוּתָ⁠א֙ דִּ֣י יְה֔וּד דִּ֥י הַיְתִ֛י מַלְכָּ֥⁠א אַ֖בִ⁠י מִן־יְהֽוּד

**"Are you Daniel, who {is} from the sons of the captivity of Judah that my father the king brought from Judah"**

The king is using the question form to express his certainty about Daniel’s identity, not to request information. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[So you are Daniel, who is from the sons of the captivity of Judah that my father the king brought from Judah!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:13
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

כְּעַ֡ן

**"And now"**

Again, Belshazzar is using the term **And now** to focus Daniel’s attention on what he is about to say. This time he will deliver his main point. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[And so]

## Daniel 5:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_fcbm)

וַ⁠אֲנָה֙ שִׁמְעֵ֣ת

**"But I, I have heard"**

Belshazzar is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I have heard** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that, unlike his wise men who were not able to interpret the writing, he himself has heard that Daniel is able to interpret it. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[But I myself have heard]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:16
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`)

## Daniel 5:16 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_j89p)

פִּשְׁרִ֛ין לְ⁠מִפְשַׁ֖ר

**"to interpret interpretations"**

The king is using a construction in which a verb and its object come from the same root: **interpret interpretations**. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this. Alternate translation: \[give interpretations of difficult things]

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:16
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`)

**"and to loosen knots"**

Belshazzar is speaking as if difficult problems were **knots** that needed to be loosened. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[untangle complicated problems] or \[solve extremely difficult problems]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

וּ⁠פִשְׁרֵ⁠הּ֙

**"and \& its interpretation"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **interpretation**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and interpret it]

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 5:16 (#6) (id: en_tn_DAN_hfjn)

אַרְגְּוָנָ֣⁠א תִלְבַּ֗שׁ ו⁠המונכ⁠א דִֽי־דַהֲבָ⁠א֙ עַֽל־צַוְּארָ֔⁠ךְ

**"you shall wear purple and a chain of gold {shall be} around your neck"**

Wearing purple clothing and a gold chain around one’s neck was a symbolic action by which the king showed that Daniel would receive great honor and authority. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[I will honor you by having you wear purple and by putting a chain of gold around your neck]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:16
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`)

## Daniel 5:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_zdys)

מַתְּנָתָ⁠ךְ֙ לָ֣⁠ךְ לֶֽהֶוְיָ֔ן וּ⁠נְבָ֥זְבְּיָתָ֖⁠ךְ לְ⁠אָחֳרָ֣ן הַ֑ב

**"Let your gifts be for yourself, and give your rewards to another"**

Daniel is saying what he wants the king to do. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants someone to do. Alternate translation: \[You may keep your gifts for yourself, and I want you to give your rewards to another]

See: Third\-Person Imperatives

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

וּ⁠פִשְׁרָ֖⁠א

**"and \& the interpretation"**

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:17
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

מַלְכוּתָ֤⁠א וּ⁠רְבוּתָ⁠א֙ וִ⁠יקָרָ֣⁠א וְ⁠הַדְרָ֔⁠ה

**"the kingdom and greatness and honor and majesty"**

## Daniel 5:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_hoqf)

עַֽמְמַיָּ֗⁠א אֻמַיָּ⁠א֙ וְ⁠לִשָּׁ֣נַיָּ֔⁠א

**"peoples, nations, and languages"**

Here, **languages** represents the people who speak those languages. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[peoples, nations, and speakers of every language]

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 5:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ulsc)

זאעין וְ⁠דָחֲלִ֖ין

**"trembling and fearing"**

The terms **trembling** and **fearing** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[extremely afraid]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:19
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 5:19 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_t5ro)

דִּֽי־הֲוָ֨ה צָבֵ֜א הֲוָ֣א קָטֵ֗ל וְ⁠דִֽי־הֲוָ֤ה צָבֵא֙ הֲוָ֣ה מַחֵ֔א וְ⁠דִֽי־הֲוָ֤ה צָבֵא֙ הֲוָ֣ה מָרִ֔ים וְ⁠דִֽי־הֲוָ֥ה צָבֵ֖א הֲוָ֥ה מַשְׁפִּֽיל

**"Whom he was wishing, he was killing, and whom he was wishing, he was letting live. And whom he was wishing, he was lifting up, and whom he was wishing, he was humbling"**

Daniel is referring to Nebuchadnezzar’s complete power over everyone by naming opposite actions: whom **he was killing** and whom **he was letting live**, and whom **he was lifting up** and whom **he was humbling**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[He had complete power over everyone, deciding whether they lived or died and whether they became important or unimportant]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:19
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 5:20 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_jtfh)

רִ֣ם לִבְבֵ֔⁠הּ וְ⁠רוּחֵ֖⁠הּ תִּֽקְפַ֣ת לַ⁠הֲזָדָ֑ה

**"his heart lifted {itself} up and his spirit became strong to be proud"**

Daniel is speaking of Nebuchadnezzar becoming proud and arrogant as if **his heart lifted** itself **up and his spirit became strong to be proud**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[he became very proud and stubbornly arrogant]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:20
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 5:21 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_usw2)

וּ⁠מִן־בְּנֵי֩ אֲנָשָׁ֨⁠א טְרִ֜יד וְ⁠לִבְבֵ֣⁠הּ ׀ עִם־חֵיוְתָ֣⁠א שוי וְ⁠עִם־עֲרָֽדַיָּ⁠א֙ מְדוֹרֵ֔⁠הּ עִשְׂבָּ֤⁠א כְ⁠תוֹרִין֙ יְטַ֣עֲמוּנֵּ֔⁠הּ וּ⁠מִ⁠טַּ֥ל שְׁמַיָּ֖⁠א גִּשְׁמֵ֣⁠הּ יִצְטַבַּ֑ע עַ֣ד דִּֽי־יְדַ֗ע דִּֽי־שַׁלִּ֞יט אֱלָהָ֤⁠א עלי⁠א בְּ⁠מַלְכ֣וּת אֲנָשָׁ֔⁠א וּ⁠לְ⁠מַן־דִּ֥י יִצְבֵּ֖ה יְהָקֵ֥ים עלי⁠ה

**"And he was driven from the sons of man, and his heart resembled with an animal, and his dwelling {was} with the wild donkeys. They fed him grass like oxen, and his body was wet with the dew of the heavens, until that he knew that the Most High God {is} ruler over the kingdom of man and whom that he wishes, he sets up over it"**

The pronoun **he** refers to Nebuchadnezzar, while the pronoun **he** in **whom that he wishes, he sets up over it** refers to God. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And Nebuchadnezzar was driven from the sons of man and his heart resembled with an animal and his dwelling was with the wild donkeys. They fed him grass like oxen and his body was wet with the dew of the heavens until Nebuchadnezzar knew that the Most High God is ruler over the kingdom of man and whomever God wishes, God sets up over it]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:21
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wild Donkey (ID: `fauna:WildDonkey`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 5:21 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_p5fx)

בְּנֵי֩ אֲנָשָׁ֨⁠א

**"the sons of man"**

Daniel is using the phrase **the sons of man** as a common expression of the culture to mean human beings in general. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[humanity]

See: Idiom

## Daniel 5:21 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_u61n)

וְ⁠לִבְבֵ֣⁠הּ ׀ עִם־חֵיוְתָ֣⁠א שוי

**"and his heart resembled with an animal"**

The point of this comparison is that just as an **animal** thinks and acts without human reason, so Nebuchadnezzar’s **heart**, or mind, became like that. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and his mind lost human reason and became like that of an animal]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 5:21
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Wild Donkey (ID: `fauna:WildDonkey`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 5:21 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_ulir)

עִשְׂבָּ֤⁠א כְ⁠תוֹרִין֙ יְטַ֣עֲמוּנֵּ֔⁠הּ

**"They fed him grass like oxen"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **oxen** eat grass, so Nebuchadnezzar was fed **grass** to eat. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[They fed him grass to eat, just as oxen eat grass]

## Daniel 5:25 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_jm8x)

מְנֵ֥א מְנֵ֖א תְּקֵ֥ל וּ⁠פַרְסִֽין

**"Mene,’ ‘Mene,’ ‘Tekel,’ and ‘Parsin"**

The words **Mene**, **Mene**, **Tekel**, and **Parsin** are words in the original language. You will need to decide if you will use these words in their original form or if you will instead translate their meaning into your language. Either approach has broad support. If you use the original words, you could spell them the way they sound in your language and then put the translation in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[‘Numbered,’ ‘Numbered,’ ‘Weighed,’ ‘Divided.’]

## Daniel - Chapter 6 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_zmff)

Daniel 6 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. King Darius appoints high officials and wants Daniel to be the highest (1–3\)
2. The other officials get Darius to make a law forbidding prayer (4–9\)
3. Daniel continues to pray to God, and the officials insist that Darius punish him (10–16\)
4. Daniel is thrown into a den of lions, but God protects him (16–23\)
5. The other officials are thrown into the den and killed, and Darius honors God (24–28\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 6:26–27\.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:0


## Daniel 6:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_xq2z)

שְׁפַר֙ קֳדָ֣ם דָּרְיָ֔וֶשׁ

**"It seemed good before Darius"**

The author is using **It seemed good before Darius** as a common expression of the culture to mean “Darius decided this.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Darius thought it best]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`)

## Daniel 6:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_xtkz)

לַ⁠אֲחַשְׁדַּרְפְּנַיָּ֖⁠א

**"satraps"**

**Satraps** were provincial governors in the ancient Persian empire, each of whom ruled one region on behalf of the king. If your readers would not be familiar with what satraps were, you could use the name of a similar kind of official that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[provincial governors]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:1
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`)

**"three sareks"**

**Sareks** were high officials who supervised the satraps in the ancient Persian empire. If your readers would not be familiar with what sareks were, you could use the name of a similar kind of official that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. לָ֧א נְהַשְׁכַּ֛ח לְ⁠דָנִיֵּ֥אל דְּנָ֖ה כָּל־עִלָּ֑א לָהֵ֕ן הַשְׁכַּ֥חְנָֽה עֲל֖וֹ⁠הִי

**"We will not find any occasion against this Daniel unless we find {something} against him"**

If it would appear in your language that these men were making a statement and then contradicting it, you could reword this as a positive statement. Alternate translation: \[The only occasion we will find against this Daniel will be something]

## Daniel 6:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_jwqx)

לְ⁠עָלְמִ֥ין חֱיִֽי

**"live to eternities"**

The officials are using **live to eternities** as a common expression of the culture to mean “may you live a very long time.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[may you live long]

* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 6:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ybzh)

יִבְעֵ֣ה בָ֠עוּ

**"petitions a petition"**

The officials are using a construction in which a verb and its object come from the same root, **petitions a petition**. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this.

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:7
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"shall be thrown into a den of lions"**

**"now the windows to him in his upper chamber were opened toward Jerusalem"**

## Daniel 6:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_unkp)

הֲ⁠לָ֧א אֱסָ֣ר רְשַׁ֗מְתָּ דִּ֣י כָל־אֱנָ֡שׁ דִּֽי־יִבְעֵה֩ מִן־כָּל־אֱלָ֨הּ וֶֽ⁠אֱנָ֜שׁ עַד \& תְּלָתִ֗ין \& מִנָּ֣⁠ךְ מַלְכָּ֔⁠א יִתְרְמֵ֕א לְ⁠ג֖וֹב אַרְיָותָ֑⁠א

**"Did you not sign a decree that any man who petitions from any god or man for thirty \& from you, O king, shall be thrown into a den of lions"**

The men are using the question form to remind the king of a decree he already made, implying that he must enforce it. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[You signed a decree that any man who petitions from any god or man for thirty days, except from you, O king, shall be thrown into a den of lions!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Medes (ID: `group:Mede`); Persians (ID: `group:Persia`); Medan (ID: `person:Medan`); Persia (ID: `place:Persia`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`)

## Daniel 6:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_tuiv)

וְ⁠עַ֧ל דָּנִיֵּ֛אל שָׂ֥ם בָּ֖ל לְ⁠שֵׁיזָבוּתֵ֑⁠הּ

**"and he set his heart on Daniel to deliver him"**

## Daniel 6:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_q625)

בֵּ⁠אדַ֜יִן מַלְכָּ֣⁠א אֲמַ֗ר וְ⁠הַיְתִיו֙ לְ⁠דָ֣נִיֵּ֔אל וּ⁠רְמ֕וֹ לְ⁠גֻבָּ֖⁠א דִּ֣י אַרְיָוָתָ֑⁠א עָנֵ֤ה מַלְכָּ⁠א֙ וְ⁠אָמַ֣ר לְ⁠דָנִיֵּ֔אל אֱלָהָ֗⁠ךְ דִּ֣י אנתה פָּֽלַֽח־לֵ⁠הּ֙ בִּ⁠תְדִירָ֔⁠א ה֖וּא יְשֵׁיזְבִנָּֽ⁠ךְ

**"Then the king was speaking, and they brought Daniel and threw {him} into a den of lions. The king answered and was saying to Daniel, “Your God, whom you are serving him in continuance, he, may he deliver you"**

You could put the information about the king speaking to Daniel before the information that they threw Daniel into the den of lions. The UST models one way to do that.

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:16
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 6:24 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_vp6a)

דִּֽי־אֲכַ֤לוּ קַרְצ֨וֹ⁠הִי֙ דִּ֣י דָֽנִיֵּ֔אל

**"who had eaten the pieces of Daniel"**

The author is using **who had eaten the pieces of Daniel** as a common expression of the culture to mean “who had maliciously accused Daniel.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Daniel 6:25 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_mxn6)

כְּ֠תַב לְֽ⁠כָל־עַֽמְמַיָּ֞⁠א אֻמַיָּ֧⁠א וְ⁠לִשָּׁנַיָּ֛⁠א דִּֽי־דארין בְּ⁠כָל־אַרְעָ֖⁠א שְׁלָמְ⁠כ֥וֹן יִשְׂגֵּֽא

**"wrote to all of the peoples, nations, and languages that were dwelling in all of the earth, “May your peace abound"**

The quotation of the letter from King Darius may begin with **to all of the peoples** rather than with **May your peace abound**. (Compare the start of the letter from King Nebuchadnezzar in 4:1\.) Alternate translation: \[wrote, “To all of the peoples, nations, and languages that are dwelling in all of the earth, may your peace abound”]

See: Direct and Indirect Quotations

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:25
* **Associated Articles:** Direct and Indirect Quotations (ID: `33305@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`)

## Daniel 6:25 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_t9nk)

וְ⁠לִשָּׁנַיָּ֛⁠א

**"and languages"**

Here, **languages** represents “the people who speak those languages.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and speakers of different languages]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:25
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`)

## Daniel 6:25 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_s5ws)

בְּ⁠כָל־אַרְעָ֖⁠א

**"in all of the earth"**

The author says **in all of the earth** as an extreme statement for emphasis. The people addressed lived throughout the king’s empire. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[throughout the entire empire]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:25
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`)

שְׁלָמְ⁠כ֥וֹן יִשְׂגֵּֽא

**"May your peace abound"**

The king is using **May your peace abound** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I wish you great well\-being.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[May you prosper greatly]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:25
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`)

**"From before me a decree is set"**

The expression **From before me a decree is set** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[I am making this decree]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:26
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 6:26 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_fiv3)

זאעין וְ⁠דָ֣חֲלִ֔ין

**"trembling and fearing"**

The terms **trembling** and **fearing** mean similar things. The king is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[deeply afraid]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:26
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 6:26 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_c8ol)

וְ⁠קַיָּם֙ לְ⁠עָ֣לְמִ֔ין

**"and enduring to eternities"**

The king is using **enduring to eternities** as a common expression of the culture to mean “existing forever.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and he exists forever]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:26
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 6:26 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_iyun)

וּ⁠מַלְכוּתֵ⁠הּ֙ דִּֽי־לָ֣א תִתְחַבַּ֔ל וְ⁠שָׁלְטָנֵ֖⁠הּ עַד־סוֹפָֽ⁠א

**"and his kingdom {is one} that will not be destroyed, and his dominion {will be} to the end"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[and his kingdom and his rule will never come to an end]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 6:26
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 6:26 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_bypg)

לָ֣א תִתְחַבַּ֔ל

**"will not be destroyed"**

## Daniel - Chapter 7 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_ffwf)

Daniel 7 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Narrative introduction: Daniel has a dream about four beasts (1\)
2. Daniel describes his dream (2–15\)
3. Daniel tells how a character in the dream explained its meaning to him (16–27\)
4. Daniel concludes this account personally (28\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 7:9–10 and 7:13–14\.

Translation issues in this chapter
----------------------------------

### Should translations try to identify what the beasts in this dream represent?

The character in the dream who explains its meaning to Daniel tells him, “These great beasts, which are four, are four kings arising from the earth” (7:17\). Interpreters have certain understandings of what kingdoms these beasts represent, for example, that the lion represents the Babylonian Empire under Nebuchadnezzar, that the bear represents the empire of the Medes and Persians, and so forth. However, we recommend that you do not put anything in the text of your translation or even in a footnote tentatively identifying these kingdoms. Their identity is not certain, and moreover it is not the task of translators to interpret the text, only to represent what it says accurately in their own languages. We recommend similarly that you not offer interpretations of the symbols in chapters 8–12\. See the discussion in the introduction to the book of Daniel, “The meaning of Daniel’s visions.”

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:0


## Daniel 7:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_eo8z)

בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֣ת חֲדָ֗ה לְ⁠בֵלְאשַׁצַּר֙ מֶ֣לֶךְ בָּבֶ֔ל

**"In the year of one to Belshazzar, the king of Babylon"**

Daniel is using the time reference **In the year of one to Belshazzar, the king of Babylon** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 7:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_s52d)

בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֣ת חֲדָ֗ה לְ⁠בֵלְאשַׁצַּר֙

**"In the year of one to Belshazzar"**

In the original language, the number here is a cardinal number, **one**, that has the sense of an ordinal number, “first.” If your language would not represent a number in a form such as **one**, you could represent this number in a form that your language would use. Alternate translation: \[In the first year of the reign of Belshazzar]

See: Ordinal Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ordinal Numbers (ID: `33393@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 7:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_z0aj)

דָּנִיֵּאל֙ חֵ֣לֶם חֲזָ֔ה וְ⁠חֶזְוֵ֥י רֵאשֵׁ֖⁠הּ עַֽל־מִשְׁכְּבֵ֑⁠הּ בֵּ⁠אדַ֨יִן֙ חֶלְמָ֣⁠א כְתַ֔ב רֵ֥אשׁ מִלִּ֖ין אֲמַֽר

**"Daniel saw a dream and visions of his head upon his bed. Then he wrote the dream; he told the head of the matters"**

Even though Daniel is the one who is reporting this vision, he is speaking of himself in the third person as **Daniel**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[I saw a dream and visions of my head upon my bed. Then I wrote the dream; I told the head of the matters]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

וְ⁠חֶזְוֵ֥י רֵאשֵׁ֖⁠הּ

**"and visions of his head"**

Daniel is using **visions of his head** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the things that he saw while he was sleeping.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and things that he saw while sleeping]

רֵ֥אשׁ מִלִּ֖ין אֲמַֽר

**"he told the head of the matters"**

Daniel is using **the head of the matters** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the most important parts of what he had seen.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Daniel 7:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_cb5p)

וְ⁠אַרְבַּ֤ע חֵיוָן֙ רַבְרְבָ֔ן סָלְקָ֖ן מִן־יַמָּ֑⁠א

## Daniel 7:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_q48r)

קַדְמָיְתָ֣⁠א כְ⁠אַרְיֵ֔ה וְ⁠גַפִּ֥ין דִּֽי־נְשַׁ֖ר לַ֑⁠הּ חָזֵ֣ה הֲוֵ֡ית עַד֩ דִּי־מְּרִ֨יטוּ גַפַּ֜י⁠הּ וּ⁠נְטִ֣ילַת מִן־אַרְעָ֗⁠א וְ⁠עַל־רַגְלַ֨יִן֙ כֶּ⁠אֱנָ֣שׁ הֳקִימַ֔ת וּ⁠לְבַ֥ב אֱנָ֖שׁ יְהִ֥יב לַֽ⁠הּ

**"The first {was} like a lion, but the wings of an eagle {were} to it. I was looking until that its wings were pulled off, and it was raised from the ground, and on two feet it was stood like a man, and the heart of a man was given to it"**

These events did not all happen at the same time; they happened one after another. Your language may have a connecting phrase that you can use to show this. Alternate translation: \[The first was like a lion, but it had the wings of an eagle. I kept watching until its wings were torn off. Next, it was lifted up from the ground. After that, it was made to stand on two feet like a man. Finally, it was given the mind of a man]

See: Connect — Sequential Time Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:4
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Sequential Time Relationship (ID: `33333@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Vulture (ID: `fauna:Vulture`)

The expressions **making afraid** and **terrible** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two expressions together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[extremely terrifying]

## Daniel 7:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_yg9r)

אֶ֤לֶף אלפים יְשַׁמְּשׁוּנֵּ֔⁠הּ וְ⁠רִבּ֥וֹ רבון קָֽדָמ֣וֹ⁠הִי יְקוּמ֑וּן

**"a thousand of thousands served him, and a myriad of myriads stood before him"**

The expressions **a thousand of thousands** and **a myriad of myriads** indicate very large numbers of beings. Translate these numbers according to the conventions of your own language and culture. Alternate translation: \[a million served him, and a hundred million stood before him]

* **Associated Articles:** Numbers (ID: `33392@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_mphf)

אֶ֤לֶף אלפים יְשַׁמְּשׁוּנֵּ֔⁠הּ וְ⁠רִבּ֥וֹ רבון קָֽדָמ֣וֹ⁠הִי יְקוּמ֑וּן

**"a thousand of thousands served him, and a myriad of myriads stood before him"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Another approach would be to connect the phrases with a word that shows that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. See how you as a team have decided to translate the parallel phrases in poetry, and follow that approach here and throughout this chapter. Alternate translation: \[an extremely large number of people served him and stood before him]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`)

## Daniel 7:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_igj3)

**"And the rest of the beasts, they took away their dominion"**

Daniel mentions **the rest of the beasts** first and then says what was done to them. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about what was done to these beasts together with the mention of the beasts themselves. ## Daniel 7:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_wvpi)

וְ⁠לֵ֨⁠הּ יְהִ֤יב שָׁלְטָן֙ וִ⁠יקָ֣ר וּ⁠מַלְכ֔וּ וְ⁠כֹ֣ל עַֽמְמַיָּ֗⁠א אֻמַיָּ֛⁠א וְ⁠לִשָּׁנַיָּ֖⁠א לֵ֣⁠הּ יִפְלְח֑וּן שָׁלְטָנֵ֞⁠הּ שָׁלְטָ֤ן עָלַם֙ דִּֽי־לָ֣א יֶעְדֵּ֔ה וּ⁠מַלְכוּתֵ֖⁠הּ דִּי־לָ֥א תִתְחַבַּֽל

**"And to him was given dominion and glory and a kingdom, that all peoples, nations, and languages should serve him. His dominion {is} a dominion of eternity that will not pass away, and his kingdom {is one} that will not be destroyed"**

Here, **that** marks **all peoples, nations, and languages should serve him** as the goal or purpose of giving him **dominion and glory and a kingdom**. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that this is the purpose.
