## Daniel 7:14 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_pywg)

שָׁלְטָנֵ֞⁠הּ שָׁלְטָ֤ן עָלַם֙ דִּֽי־לָ֣א יֶעְדֵּ֔ה וּ⁠מַלְכוּתֵ֖⁠הּ דִּי־לָ֥א תִתְחַבַּֽל

**"His dominion {is} a dominion of eternity that will not pass away, and his kingdom {is one} that will not be destroyed"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[His royal rule will certainly endure forever]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:14 (#6) (id: en_tn_DAN_ucgy)

שָׁלְטָ֤ן עָלַם֙

**"is} a dominion of eternity"**

Daniel is using the possessive form to describe a **dominion** that is characterized by **eternity**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[is a dominion that lasts forever]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:14 (#7) (id: en_tn_DAN_mrrj)

דִּי־לָ֥א תִתְחַבַּֽל

**"is one} that will not be destroyed"**

The expression **will not be destroyed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[is one that nothing will ever destroy]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_o1x3)

אֶתְכְּרִיַּ֥ת רוּחִ֛⁠י אֲנָ֥ה דָנִיֵּ֖אל בְּ⁠ג֣וֹא נִדְנֶ֑ה

**"My spirit was distressed, I, Daniel, in the midst of the body"**

Daniel interrupts his statement about his spirit in order to identify himself. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about **I, Daniel** before the information about his spirit being distressed. Alternate translation: \[As for me, Daniel, my spirit within my body was distressed]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 7:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_c9zp)

אֶתְכְּרִיַּ֥ת רוּחִ֛⁠י אֲנָ֥ה דָנִיֵּ֖אל בְּ⁠ג֣וֹא נִדְנֶ֑ה

**"My spirit was distressed, I, Daniel, in the midst of the body"**

Daniel is using **My spirit was distressed** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I was deeply troubled within myself.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I, Daniel, was deeply troubled]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

**"one of the ones standing"**

Here Daniel is introducing **one of the ones standing** as a new participant in the story. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[a certain man who was standing there]

**"and he made me know the interpretation of the matters"**

## Daniel 7:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_fuef)

**"These great beasts"**

This is the beginning of what the **one of the ones standing** told Daniel. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could introduce this as a direct quotation of him. Alternate translation: \[He told me, ‘These great beasts]

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_mcy6)

**"These great beasts, which are four, {are} four kings arising from the earth"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[He told me that those great beasts, which were four, were four kings arising from the earth]

## Daniel 7:17 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_coi2)

אַרְבְּעָ֥ה מַלְכִ֖ין יְקוּמ֥וּן מִן־אַרְעָֽ⁠א

**"are} four kings arising from the earth"**

Here, **four kings** represent the four kingdoms that these kings rule. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[are four kingdoms that will arise on the earth]

## Daniel 7:17 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_jw14)

יְקוּמ֥וּן מִן־אַרְעָֽ⁠א

**"arising from the earth"**

The attendant is using **arising from the earth** as a common expression of the culture to mean “coming to power in the world.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[who will come to power in the world]

## Daniel 7:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_osrf)

וִֽ⁠יקַבְּלוּן֙

**"But \& will receive"**

The attendant uses the word **But** here to indicate a contrast between the four kings who arise from the earth and the holy people of God who receive the kingdom. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[However,]

**"But the saints of the Most High will receive the kingdom"**

## Daniel 7:18 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_ihoc)

עַֽד־עָ֣לְמָ֔⁠א וְ⁠עַ֖ד עָלַ֥ם עָלְמַיָּֽ⁠א

**"to eternity and to eternity of eternities"**

The expressions **to eternity** and **to eternity of eternities** mean similar things. The attendant is using the two expressions together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[forever without end]

## Daniel 7:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ggqd)

דִּֽי־הֲוָ֥ת שָֽׁנְיָ֖ה מִן־כל⁠הון דְּחִילָ֣ה יַתִּ֗ירָה שני⁠ה דִּֽי־פַרְזֶל֙ וְ⁠טִפְרַ֣י⁠הּ דִּֽי־נְחָ֔שׁ אָֽכְלָ֣ה מַדֲּקָ֔ה וּ⁠שְׁאָרָ֖⁠א בְּ⁠רַגְלַ֥י⁠הּ רָֽפְסָֽה

**"which was being different from all of them, making afraid exceedingly, its teeth of iron and its claws of bronze, devouring, crushing, and trampling the rest with its feet"**

Daniel gives a long series of descriptions of **the fourth beast** within one sentence. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about what this beast looked like and what it did into separate sentences. Alternate translation: \[which was different from all of them. It was exceedingly frightening. Its teeth were iron and its claws were bronze. It devoured and crushed, and it trampled the rest with its feet]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:19
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:23 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_buzv)

כֵּן֮ אֲמַר֒ חֵֽיוְתָ⁠א֙ רְבִיעָ֣יְתָ֔⁠א מַלְכ֤וּ רביעי⁠א תֶּהֱוֵ֣א בְ⁠אַרְעָ֔⁠א

**"Thus he was saying, ‘The fourth beast will be a fourth kingdom on the earth"**

There is a direct quotation inside a direct quotation here. If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[Thus he was saying that the fourth beast will be a fourth kingdom on the earth]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:23
* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:23 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_v4k4)

כֵּן֮ אֲמַר֒

**"Thus he was saying"**

The pronoun **he** refers to the one standing whom Daniel approached, as verse 16 describes. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Thus the one standing there was saying]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:23
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:23 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_mrit)

וְ⁠תֵאכֻל֙ כָּל־אַרְעָ֔⁠א וּ⁠תְדוּשִׁנַּ֖⁠הּ וְ⁠תַדְּקִנַּֽ⁠הּ

**"And it will devour all of the earth, and it will trample it and crush it"**

Here the attendant is speaking as if this kingdom were a beast that would **devour** the nations it conquered and **trample** and **crush** them. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And it will conquer all of the earth, and it will defeat and destroy every nation]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

כָּל־אַרְעָ֔⁠א

**"all of the earth"**

The attendant says **all of the earth** as an extreme statement for emphasis. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[a vast expanse of land]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:23
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:24 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_qio9)

וְ⁠קַרְנַיָּ֣⁠א עֲשַׂ֔ר מִנַּ⁠הּ֙ מַלְכוּתָ֔⁠ה עַשְׂרָ֥ה מַלְכִ֖ין יְקֻמ֑וּן

**"And the ten horns, from this kingdom, ten kings will arise"**

The attendant mentions **the ten horns** first and then explains in a new clause what they represent. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about the ten kings together with the mention of the ten horns. Alternate translation: \[And from this kingdom, ten kings, who are the ten horns, will arise]

* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:24 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_h7h0)

וְ⁠ה֤וּא יִשְׁנֵא֙ מִן־קַדְמָיֵ֔⁠א

**"And he will be different from the first {ones"**

The attendant is using the adjective **first** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[And he will be different from the kings who ruled before him]

## Daniel 7:25 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_nnhj)

וּ⁠מִלִּ֗ין לְ⁠צַ֤ד עלי⁠א יְמַלִּ֔ל

**"And he will speak words against the Most High"**

The implication is that these **words against the Most High** are defiant and insulting. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[And he will speak arrogant insults against the Most High]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:25 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_nn29)

עלי⁠א

**"the Most High"**

The first instance of **the Most High** is singular, while the second instance is plural.
This is an Aramaic plural of excellence. It does not mean that there is more than one God, but that the one true God is supremely glorious and powerful. If your language can use its plural form for intensification, not just for number, it would be appropriate to use it here. Other languages may have other ways to show this intensification. Alternate translation: \[the Most High … the Almighty God]

See: Unusual Uses of the Plural

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Unusual Uses of the Plural (ID: `33395@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 7:25 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_tvo6)

וּ⁠לְ⁠קַדִּישֵׁ֥י עֶלְיוֹנִ֖ין יְבַלֵּ֑א

**"and he will wear out the saints of the Most High"**

Here the attendant is speaking as if oppressing God’s holy people relentlessly were to **wear out** a garment until it fell apart. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and he will continually afflict the holy people of the Most High until they are exhausted]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 7:25 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_mopm)

לְ⁠הַשְׁנָיָה֙ זִמְנִ֣ין וְ⁠דָ֔ת

**"to change the times and the law"**

The implication is that **the times** are the festivals that God appointed for his people and that **the law** is the law that God gave them. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[to change the appointed festivals and the law that God gave his people]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

**"And they will be given into his hand"**

The expression **will be given** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And God will put the times and the law into his hand]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

וְ⁠יִתְיַהֲב֣וּן בִּ⁠ידֵ֔⁠הּ

**"And they will be given into his hand"**

The pronoun **they** refers to the **saints of the Most High**, not to **the times and the law**. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And the saints will be given]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

וְ⁠יִתְיַהֲב֣וּן בִּ⁠ידֵ֔⁠הּ

**"And they will be given into his hand"**

Here, **his hand** represents the power and control of this king. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And they will be given over to his power]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:25
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:25 (#8) (id: en_tn_DAN_v99h)

עַד־עִדָּ֥ן וְ⁠עִדָּנִ֖ין וּ⁠פְלַ֥ג עִדָּֽן

**"for a time and times and half of a time"**

The attendant is using **a time and times and half of a time** as a common expression of the culture to mean “a limited period that God has determined.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[for a limited period that God has determined]

## Daniel 7:26 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_t6cp)

לְ⁠הַשְׁמָדָ֥ה וּ⁠לְ⁠הוֹבָדָ֖ה עַד־סוֹפָֽ⁠א

**"to consume and destroy {it} to the end"**

Here, **to** marks **consume and destroy** as the goal or purpose of taking away his dominion. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that this is the purpose. Alternate translation: \[totally consuming and destroying it]

See: Connect — Goal (Purpose) Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:26
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Goal (Purpose) Relationship (ID: `33330@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:26 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_vgwk)

לְ⁠הַשְׁמָדָ֥ה וּ⁠לְ⁠הוֹבָדָ֖ה

**"to consume and destroy {it"**

The terms **consume** and **destroy** mean similar things. The attendant is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[to utterly destroy it]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:26
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:26 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_or8b)

עַד־סוֹפָֽ⁠א

**"to the end"**

The attendant is using **to the end** as a common expression of the culture to mean “completely and finally.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[completely and finally]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:26
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 7:27 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_adi6)

וּ⁠מַלְכוּתָ֨⁠ה וְ⁠שָׁלְטָנָ֜⁠א וּ⁠רְבוּתָ֗⁠א דִּ֚י מַלְכְוָת֙

**"and the kingdom and the dominion and the greatness of the kingdoms"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **the kingdom**, **the dominion**, and **the greatness**, you could express the same ideas in another way. Alternate translation: \[and the right to reign and to rule gloriously over the kingdoms]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:27
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 7:27 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_x1yx)

תְּח֣וֹת כָּל־שְׁמַיָּ֔⁠א

**"under all of the heavens"**

The attendant says **under all of the heavens** as an extreme statement for emphasis. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[throughout the entire earth]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:27
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 7:27 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_xk3s)

יְהִיבַ֕ת לְ⁠עַ֖ם קַדִּישֵׁ֣י עֶלְיוֹנִ֑ין

**"will be given to the people of the saints of the Most High"**

The expression **will be given** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[God will give to his people, the saints of the Most High]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:27
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:27 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_pcdi)

וְ⁠כֹל֙ שָׁלְטָ֣נַיָּ֔⁠א לֵ֥⁠הּ יִפְלְח֖וּן וְ⁠יִֽשְׁתַּמְּעֽוּן

**"and all dominions will serve and make themselves hear him"**

Here, **all dominions** represent the rulers who hold that authority. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and all rulers will serve and make themselves hear him]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:27
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 7:27 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_kr32)

לֵ֥⁠הּ יִפְלְח֖וּן וְ⁠יִֽשְׁתַּמְּעֽוּן

**"will serve and make themselves hear him"**

In this context, the term **hear** has the sense of “obey.” While the form is reflexive in the original, in many languages an active form will convey the meaning accurately. Alternate translation: \[will serve and obey him]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:27
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Daniel 7:27 (#6) (id: en_tn_DAN_afe8)

לֵ֥⁠הּ יִפְלְח֖וּן וְ⁠יִֽשְׁתַּמְּעֽוּן

**"will serve and make themselves hear him"**

The terms **serve** and **make themselves hear him** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[will diligently obey him] or \[will obey him completely]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:27
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:28 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_b1r5)

עַד־כָּ֖ה סוֹפָ֣⁠א דִֽי־מִלְּתָ֑⁠א

**"To here {is} the end of the matter"**

The words **To here** signal the end of Daniel’s report of what he saw in his vision. Use the natural form in your language for expressing the conclusion of an account. Alternate translation: \[That is the end of my report]

See: End of Story

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:28
* **Associated Articles:** End of Story (ID: `33417@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 7:28 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_nsws)

וְ⁠זִיוַ⁠י֙ יִשְׁתַּנּ֣וֹן עֲלַ֔⁠י

**"and my splendors changed upon me"**

Daniel is using **my splendors changed upon me** as a common expression of the culture to mean “my face turned pale.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and my face turned pale]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:28

## Daniel 7:28 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_rbd2)

וּ⁠מִלְּתָ֖⁠א בְּ⁠לִבִּ֥⁠י נִטְרֵֽת

**"But I kept the matter in my heart"**

Here Daniel is speaking as if his **heart** were a container in which he could keep something. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[But I did not tell anyone what I had seen]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 7:28
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel - Chapter 8 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_vldp)

Daniel 8 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Daniel sees a vision of a ram, a goat, and a horn that became great (1–14\)
2. The angel Gabriel explains the vision to Daniel (15–27\)

Translation issues in this chapter
----------------------------------

### The meaning of the animals in this vision

In the case of this vision, the interpretation that a figure gives to Daniel does specify what the animals represent. Gabriel tells Daniel, “The ram that you saw … is the kings of Media and Persia” (8:20\) and “The male goat is the king of Javan” (that is, Greece, 8:21\). There is no reason not to include this specific information in your translation. However, the identity of the king in verses 23–25 is not certain, and we recommend that you not suggest in your translation who this might be.

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:0


## Daniel 8:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_mosu)

בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֣ת שָׁל֔וֹשׁ לְ⁠מַלְכ֖וּת

**"In the year of three to the reign of"**

The expression **In the year of three** uses a number in a form that means the third year of Belshazzar’s reign. If your language would not represent a number in a form such as this, you could represent this number in a form that your language would use. Alternate translation: \[In the third year of the reign of]

See: Ordinal Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ordinal Numbers (ID: `33393@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_kcud)

בֵּלְאשַׁצַּ֣ר הַ⁠מֶּ֑לֶךְ

**"King Belshazzar"**

The word **Belshazzar** is the name of a man.

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:1
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_m67z)

חָז֞וֹן נִרְאָ֤ה אֵלַ⁠י֙ אֲנִ֣י דָנִיֵּ֔אל

**"a vision appeared to me, me, Daniel"**

Daniel is stating the pronoun **me** separately, even though the expression **to me** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that the vision he is describing is one that he saw personally. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[to me, Daniel, personally]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:1
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_iaap)

אֵלַ⁠י֙ אֲנִ֣י דָנִיֵּ֔אל

**"to me, me, Daniel"**

Daniel is repeating forms of the word **me** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[to me indeed, Daniel]

See: Reduplication

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:1 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_giwl)

אַחֲרֵ֛י הַ⁠נִּרְאָ֥ה אֵלַ֖⁠י בַּ⁠תְּחִלָּֽה

**"after the one having appeared to me at the first"**

The implication is that **the one having appeared to me at the first** refers to the vision that Daniel describes in the previous chapter. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[after the earlier vision that had appeared to me, the one I have just described]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:1
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Belshazzar (ID: `person:Belshazzar`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_d1bj)

בְּ⁠שׁוּשַׁ֣ן הַ⁠בִּירָ֔ה אֲשֶׁ֖ר בְּ⁠עֵילָ֣ם הַ⁠מְּדִינָ֑ה

**"was} in Susa the citadel, which {is} in Elam the province"**

The word **Susa** is the name of a city, and the word **Elam** is the name of a province. Later in this verse, **Ulai** is the name of a canal.

Daniel is using **I lifted up my eyes** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I looked up.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. וְ⁠הָ⁠אַחַת֙ גְּבֹהָ֣ה מִן־הַ⁠שֵּׁנִ֔ית וְ⁠הַ֨⁠גְּבֹהָ֔ה עֹלָ֖ה בָּ⁠אַחֲרֹנָֽה

Daniel is using the adjectives **the one**, **the second**, and **the higher** as nouns to mean certain horns. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. ## Daniel 8:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_iu6z)

אֶת־שְׁתֵּ֣י קְרָנָ֔י⁠ו

**"the two of its horns"**

In this instance, the term **horns** is plural, not dual as in verses 3 and 6\. Translate this in the way your language would naturally express numbers.

See: Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Numbers (ID: `33392@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Daniel 8:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_tw9u)

מִ⁠יָּדֽ⁠וֹ

**"from its hand"**

Here, **hand** represents the power/control of the goat. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[from the goat’s power]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Daniel 8:7 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_e8nz)

וְ⁠לֹא־הָ֥יָה כֹ֛חַ בָּ⁠אַ֖יִל לַ⁠עֲמֹ֣ד לְ⁠פָנָ֑י⁠ו

**"And the ram was not strong to stand to its face"**

Daniel is using **stand to its face** as a common expression of the culture to mean “resist it.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[was not strong enough to resist the goat] or \[could not withstand the goat]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Daniel 8:7 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_hwnf)

וַ⁠יַּשְׁלִיכֵ֤⁠הוּ אַ֨רְצָ⁠ה֙ וַֽ⁠יִּרְמְסֵ֔⁠הוּ

**"And it cast it to the earth and trampled it"**

The first pronoun **it** in this sentence refers to the goat, and the pronouns that follow refer to the ram. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And the goat cast the ram to the earth and trampled it]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Daniel 8:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_c5ea)

נִשְׁבְּרָה֙ הַ⁠קֶּ֣רֶן הַ⁠גְּדוֹלָ֔ה

**"the great horn was broken"**

The expression **was broken** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[something broke off the great horn]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

## Daniel 8:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_cpfe)

לְ⁠אַרְבַּ֖ע רוּח֥וֹת הַ⁠שָּׁמָֽיִם

**"toward the four winds of the heavens"**

Daniel is speaking as if the four directions were **the four winds of the heavens**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[toward the four directions of the earth]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

## Daniel 8:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_kqxt)

וְ⁠אֶל־הַ⁠צֶּֽבִי

**"and toward the Beauty"**

Here, **the Beauty** represents “the land of Israel.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and toward the glorious land of Israel]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Daniel 8:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_pm19)

צְבָ֣א הַ⁠שָּׁמָ֑יִם

**"the army of the heavens"**

Daniel is speaking of the beings who live in heaven as if they were **the army of the heavens**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the heavenly beings]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:10
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 8:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_ak7e)

שַֽׂר־הַ⁠צָּבָ֖א

**"the commander of the army"**

The implication is that **the commander of the army** is God, who rules over the beings who live in heaven. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[God, who commands the army of heaven]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:11
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 8:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_sepm)

וּ⁠מִמֶּ֨⁠נּוּ֙ הרים הַ⁠תָּמִ֔יד וְ⁠הֻשְׁלַ֖ךְ מְכ֥וֹן מִקְדָּשֽׁ⁠וֹ

**"And the perpetual sacrifice was removed from him, and the place of his sanctuary was cast down"**

The expressions **was removed** and **was cast down** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And it took away the perpetual sacrifice from him, and it threw down the place of his sanctuary]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:11
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 8:11 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_b3wz)

הַ⁠תָּמִ֔יד

**"the perpetual sacrifice"**

The implication is that **the perpetual sacrifice** is the offering that the priests presented to God every morning and every evening. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the sacrifice that the priests offered to God every morning and evening]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:11
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 8:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_qrqe)

תִּנָּתֵ֥ן

**"will be given over"**

The expression **will be given over** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[God will hand an army over]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:12
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`)

## Daniel 8:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_m39d)

וְ⁠תַשְׁלֵ֤ךְ אֱמֶת֙ אַ֔רְצָ⁠ה

**"and it will cast truth to the ground"**

Daniel is speaking as if truth were an object that could be thrown down when he says that the horn **will cast truth to the ground**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and it will utterly reject the truth]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:12
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`)

## Daniel 8:12 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_w8nf)

וְ⁠עָשְׂתָ֖ה וְ⁠הִצְלִֽיחָה

**"And it will do and succeed"**

The phrase **do and succeed** expresses a single idea. The word **succeed** tells more about **do**. If it would be more natural in your language, you could express this meaning in a different way. Alternate translation: \[And it will do whatever it wishes with success]

See: Hendiadys

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:12
* **Associated Articles:** Hendiadys (ID: `33280@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`)

## Daniel 8:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_jkx2)

וָ⁠אֶשְׁמְעָ֥⁠ה אֶֽחָד־קָד֖וֹשׁ מְדַבֵּ֑ר

**"And I heard a holy one speaking"**

The implication is that **a holy one** is an angel. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[And I heard a holy angel speaking]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:13
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Daniel 8:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_qorl)

תֵּ֛ת וְ⁠קֹ֥דֶשׁ וְ⁠צָבָ֖א מִרְמָֽס

**"the giving {of} both the sanctuary and the army for trampling"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **the giving**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[that God will give over for trampling both the sanctuary and the army]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:13
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Daniel 8:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_gst2)

מִרְמָֽס

**"for trampling"**

The holy one is speaking of the defeat and desecration of the sanctuary and the army as if enemies would crush them underfoot, since they have been given **for trampling**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[to be crushed by enemies]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:13
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Daniel 8:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_elmh)

וַ⁠יֹּ֣אמֶר אֵלַ֔⁠י

**"And he said to me"**

The pronoun **he** refers to the holy one who was speaking, whom Daniel mentioned first in the previous verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And that first holy one said to me]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:14
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`)

## Daniel 8:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_z8g4)

אֵלַ֔⁠י

**"to me"**

Some ancient manuscripts read “to me.” The ULT follows that reading. Other ancient manuscripts read “to him.” If a translation of the Bible exists in your region, you may wish to use the reading that it uses. If a translation of the Bible does not exist in your region, you may wish to use the reading of the ULT.

See: Textual Variants

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:14
* **Associated Articles:** Textual Variants (ID: `33405@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`)

## Daniel 8:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_k6y8)

עַ֚ד עֶ֣רֶב בֹּ֔קֶר אַלְפַּ֖יִם וּ⁠שְׁלֹ֣שׁ מֵא֑וֹת

**"Until evening, morning, 2,300"**

The number **2,300** counts pairs of an evening and a morning, that is, the times when the priests presented the perpetual sacrifice. Translate this number according to the conventions of your own language and culture. Alternate translation: \[Until 2,300 evenings and mornings have passed]

See: Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:14
* **Associated Articles:** Numbers (ID: `33392@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`)

## Daniel 8:14 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_cpr9)

עַ֚ד עֶ֣רֶב בֹּ֔קֶר

**"Until evening, morning"**

The holy one is referring to all of each day by naming two major components, **evening** and **morning**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[Until there have passed full days numbering]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:14
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`)

## Daniel 8:14 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_gdzq)

וְ⁠נִצְדַּ֖ק קֹֽדֶשׁ

**"Then the sanctuary will be made right"**

The expression **will be made right** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[Then God will make the sanctuary right]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:14
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`)

## Daniel 8:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_i2kv)

וְ⁠הִנֵּ֛ה

**"And behold"**

Daniel is using the term **behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[And listen]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_gqhi)

וְ⁠הִנֵּ֛ה עֹמֵ֥ד לְ⁠נֶגְדִּ֖⁠י

**"And behold, one standing to my face"**

Daniel is introducing **one standing to my face** as a new participant in the story. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[And suddenly there was someone standing in front of me]

See: Introduction of New and Old Participants

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:15
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of New and Old Participants (ID: `33421@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_uf8n)

וְ⁠הִנֵּ֛ה עֹמֵ֥ד לְ⁠נֶגְדִּ֖⁠י

**"And behold, one standing to my face"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. It may be helpful to supply these words from the context. Alternate translation: \[behold, I saw one standing in front of me]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:15
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:15 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_hsfo)

כְּ⁠מַרְאֵה־גָֽבֶר

**"according to the appearance of a man"**

Daniel says that this being was **according to the appearance of a man**. The point of this comparison is that the one standing before him looked like a human being, although he was not actually a man. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[who looked like a human being, although he was not one]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:15
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Daniel 8:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_zan0)

בֵּ֣ין אוּלָ֑י

**"between the Ulai"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[coming from between the banks of the Ulai]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:16
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ulai (ID: `place:Ulai`); Gabriel (ID: `deity:Gabriel`); Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Daniel 8:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ck4k)

הָבֵ֥ן לְ⁠הַלָּ֖ז אֶת־הַ⁠מַּרְאֶֽה

**"make this one understand the appearance"**

The pronoun **this one** refers to Daniel. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[make Daniel understand the appearance]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:16
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ulai (ID: `place:Ulai`); Gabriel (ID: `deity:Gabriel`); Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Daniel 8:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_hpk9)

וַ⁠יָּבֹא֙ אֵ֣צֶל עָמְדִ֔⁠י

**"And he came beside my standing"**

Daniel is using **beside my standing** as a common expression of the culture to mean “to the place where I was standing.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And he came to the place where I was standing]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:17
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Daniel 8:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_om61)

וָ⁠אֶפְּלָ֖⁠ה עַל־פָּנָ֑⁠י

**"and fell on my face"**

Falling face down on the ground was a symbolic action by which Daniel showed fear and reverence in the presence of this heavenly messenger. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[and fell down with my face to the ground to show reverence]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:17
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Daniel 8:17 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_h8bo)

בֶּן־אָדָ֔ם

**"son of man"**

Gabriel is using **son of man** as a common expression of the culture to mean “mortal human being.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[human being]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:17
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Perceive (ID: `keyterm:Perceive`)

## Daniel 8:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_jtxp)

הִנְ⁠נִ֣י מוֹדִֽיעֲ⁠ךָ֔

**"Behold me, making you know"**

Gabriel is using **Behold me, making you know** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I am about to make you know.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I am about to tell you]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:19
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 8:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_sghn)

בְּ⁠אַחֲרִ֣ית הַ⁠זָּ֑עַם

**"at the end of the indignation"**

The implication is that **the indignation** is God’s anger against sinful people. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[at the end of God’s anger against sinners]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:19
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Daniel 8:20 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_bgm3)

הָ⁠אַ֥יִל אֲשֶׁר־רָאִ֖יתָ בַּ֣עַל הַ⁠קְּרָנָ֑יִם מַלְכֵ֖י מָדַ֥י וּ⁠פָרָֽס

**"The ram that you saw, a master of two horns, {is} the kings of Media and Persia"**

Gabriel is speaking as if **The ram** that Daniel saw were the same thing as the kings of Media and Persia, when he means that the ram represents them. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The ram that you saw, a master of two horns, represents the kings of Media and Persia]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:20
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Persia (ID: `place:Persia`); Media (ID: `place:Media`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Daniel 8:21 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_lqn0)

יָוָ֑ן

**"Javan"**

The word **Javan** is the name of a people group. The home of those people is known in many languages today as “Greece.” You could use that name in your translation if this would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Greece]

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:21
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Greece (ID: `place:Greece`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

## Daniel 8:21 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_sa62)

וְ⁠הַ⁠קֶּ֤רֶן הַ⁠גְּדוֹלָה֙ אֲשֶׁ֣ר בֵּין־עֵינָ֔י⁠ו ה֖וּא הַ⁠מֶּ֥לֶךְ הָ⁠רִאשֽׁוֹן

**"and the great horn that {was} between its eyes, it {is} the first king"**

Gabriel names **the great horn** first and then refers back to it with the pronoun **it**. If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about the horn and the information about the king together in a single statement. Alternate translation: \[and the great horn that was between its eyes represents the first king]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:21
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Greece (ID: `place:Greece`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`)

## Daniel 8:22 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_dzpx)

וְ⁠לֹ֥א בְ⁠כֹחֽ⁠וֹ

**"but not with its power"**

Gabriel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[but they will not stand with the power it has]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 8:22
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`)

## Daniel 8:23 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_lb40)

וּֽ⁠בְ⁠אַחֲרִית֙ מַלְכוּתָ֔⁠ם

**"And at the end of their rule"**

The pronoun **their** refers to the four kingdoms that Gabriel described in the previous verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And at the end of these kingdoms’ rule]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

## Daniel 8:23 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_t9t1)

כְּ⁠הָתֵ֖ם הַ⁠פֹּשְׁעִ֑ים

**"when transgressors are complete"**

Gabriel is using **when transgressors are complete** as a common expression of the culture to mean “when sinful people have done all the evil that they will do.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[when sinful people have finished doing all their evil]

## Daniel 8:23 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_ksyb)

הַ⁠פֹּשְׁעִ֑ים

**"transgressors"**

Some ancient manuscripts read “transgressors.” The ULT follows that reading. Other ancient manuscripts read “transgressions.” If a translation of the Bible exists in your region, you may wish to use the reading that it uses. If a translation of the Bible does not exist in your region, you may wish to use the reading of the ULT.

**"And his power will be mighty, but not by his power"**

## Daniel - Chapter 9 Introduction (id: en_tn_DAN_c9e0)

Daniel 9 Introduction
=====================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

1. Daniel prays, based on Jeremiah’s prophecy, that God will restore Jerusalem (1–19\)
2. Gabriel comes and explains a further meaning of Jeremiah’s prophecy (20–27\)

Translation issues in this chapter
----------------------------------

### What are the “seventy sevens” that Gabriel describes (9:24\)?

The Hebrew word for “seven” can also mean a “week,” that is, a period of seven days, and so some translators say “seventy weeks” here. Some interpreters understand the term in this context to mean a “week of years,” that is, seven years, and so some translations say “490 years.” However, these meanings are not certain. It is also possible that the number seven is being used to symbolize completeness rather than to indicate a specific total of years. So we encourage translators to use general expressions that allow for the range of possible interpretations rather than naming a specific total such as 490\. The UST models one way to do this. It translates “seventy sevens” as “seventy sets of seven time periods.”

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:0


## Daniel 9:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_rg9m)

בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֣ת אַחַ֗ת לְ⁠דָרְיָ֛וֶשׁ

**"In the year of one to Darius"**

The original language uses a cardinal number, **one**, where your language might use an ordinal number. If your language would not represent a number in a form such as **In the year of one to Darius**, you could represent this number in a form that your language would use. Alternate translation: \[In the first year of Darius]

See: Ordinal Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ordinal Numbers (ID: `33393@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Darius (ID: `person:Darius.3`); Medes (ID: `group:Mede`); Medan (ID: `person:Medan`); Chaldea (ID: `place:Chaldea`); Ahasuerus (ID: `person:Ahasuerus`); Chaldeans (ID: `group:Chaldea`)

**"the desolations of Jerusalem"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **desolations**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the days in which Jerusalem would lie devastated]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:2
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_a7nj)

וָ⁠אֶתְּנָ֣⁠ה אֶת־פָּנַ֗⁠י אֶל־אֲדֹנָ⁠י֙ הָֽ⁠אֱלֹהִ֔ים

**"And I set my face to the Lord God"**

Daniel is using **I set my face to the Lord God** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I gave my full attention to the Lord God.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And I turned my full attention to the Lord God]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:3
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plea for Mercy (ID: `keyterm:Supplication`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

## Daniel 9:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_kmq2)

תְּפִלָּ֖ה וְ⁠תַחֲנוּנִ֑ים

**"by} prayer and supplications"**

The terms **prayer** and **supplications** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[by earnest pleading]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:3
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plea for Mercy (ID: `keyterm:Supplication`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

## Daniel 9:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_pae7)

בְּ⁠צ֖וֹם וְ⁠שַׂ֥ק וָ⁠אֵֽפֶר

**"with fasting and sackcloth and ashes"**

Going without food, wearing rough cloth, and putting ashes on oneself were symbolic actions by which Daniel showed that he was mourning over sin and humbling himself before God. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of these actions in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[with fasting and sackcloth and ashes to show that I was mourning and humbling myself]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:3
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plea for Mercy (ID: `keyterm:Supplication`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

## Daniel 9:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_s3t9)

וָ⁠אֵֽפֶר

**"and ashes"**

In this verse, the word **ashes** is singular in form, but it refers to all of the particles of ashes as a group. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use the plural form of “ashes.” Alternate translation: \[and ash particles]

See: Collective Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:3
* **Associated Articles:** Collective Nouns (ID: `33324@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plea for Mercy (ID: `keyterm:Supplication`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sackcloth (ID: `realia:Sackcloth`)

## Daniel 9:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_vtay)

וָֽ⁠אֶתְפַּֽלְלָ֛⁠ה לַ⁠יהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהַ֖⁠י וָ⁠אֶתְוַדֶּ֑ה וָ⁠אֹֽמְרָ֗⁠ה

**"And I prayed to Yahweh my God, and I confessed and said"**

Daniel is using three verbs, **I prayed**, **I confessed**, and **said**, to introduce a single direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

אָנָּ֤א אֲדֹנָ⁠י֙

**"Oh Lord"**

Daniel is using a phrase that expresses a strong feeling of urgent appeal to God. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[Please, Lord]

**"the great and feared God"**

The terms **great** and **feared** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[the awesome God]

**"the one keeping covenant and covenant faithfulness"**

The terms **keeping covenant** and **covenant faithfulness** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[the one faithfully keeping his covenant]

## Daniel 9:4 (#5) (id: en_tn_DAN_n2zu)

הַ⁠בְּרִית֙ וְֽ⁠הַ⁠חֶ֔סֶד

**"covenant and covenant faithfulness"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **covenant** and **faithfulness**, you could express the same ideas in another way. Alternate translation: \[what he promised, and acting faithfully]

## Daniel 9:4 (#6) (id: en_tn_DAN_xx9a)

לְ⁠אֹהֲבָ֖י⁠ו וּ⁠לְ⁠שֹׁמְרֵ֥י מִצְוֺתָֽי⁠ו

**"to the ones loving him and to the keepers of his commandments"**

Even though Daniel is addressing God directly, he is speaking of him in the third person as **him** and **his** to show respect. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in a respectful form in the second person. Alternate translation: \[to the ones loving you and to the keepers of your commandments]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:4
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:4 (#7) (id: en_tn_DAN_tmuc)

לְ⁠אֹהֲבָ֖י⁠ו וּ⁠לְ⁠שֹׁמְרֵ֥י מִצְוֺתָֽי⁠ו

**"to the ones loving him and to the keepers of his commandments"**

These two phrases mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[to the ones who show that they truly love him by keeping his commandments]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:4
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_wr8j)

חָטָ֥אנוּ

**"we have sinned"**

By **we**, Daniel means himself and his fellow Israelites but not the one to whom he is speaking, so use the exclusive form of that word in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

See: Exclusive and Inclusive ‘We’

* **Associated Articles:** Exclusive and Inclusive ‘We’ (ID: `33271@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"and committed iniquity"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **iniquity**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and acted perversely]

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_mjx4)

וְ⁠ס֥וֹר מִ⁠מִּצְוֺתֶ֖⁠ךָ וּ⁠מִ⁠מִּשְׁפָּטֶֽי⁠ךָ

**"turning away from your commandments and your judgments"**

Daniel is speaking as if refusing to obey God’s commands were **turning away** from them. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[refusing to obey your commandments and your judgments]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

מִ⁠מִּצְוֺתֶ֖⁠ךָ וּ⁠מִ⁠מִּשְׁפָּטֶֽי⁠ךָ

**"from your commandments and your judgments"**

The terms **your commandments** and **your judgments** mean similar things. Daniel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[turning away from all of your laws]

וְ⁠לֹ֤א שָׁמַ֨עְנוּ֙ אֶל־עֲבָדֶ֣י⁠ךָ הַ⁠נְּבִיאִ֔ים

**"And we have not listened to your servants the prophets"**

Alternate translation: \[And we have not obeyed your servants the prophets]

לִ⁠מְלָכֵ֥י⁠נוּ לְ⁠שָׂרֵ֖י⁠נוּ וְ⁠לַ⁠אֲבֹתֵ֑י⁠נוּ

**"to our kings, to our princes, and to our fathers"**

Daniel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[to our kings, our princes, and our fathers there is also complete disgrace]

## Daniel 9:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_lvge)

וַ⁠תִּתַּ֨ךְ עָלֵ֜י⁠נוּ הָ⁠אָלָ֣ה וְ⁠הַ⁠שְּׁבֻעָ֗ה אֲשֶׁ֤ר כְּתוּבָה֙ בְּ⁠תוֹרַת֙ מֹשֶׁ֣ה

**"And the oath has been poured out on us, and the curse, that is written in the law of Moses"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about **the curse** with the information about **the oath**. Alternate translation: \[And the oath and the curse have been poured out on us, which are written in the law of Moses]

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:11
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Daniel 9:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_kjtl)

וַ⁠תִּתַּ֨ךְ עָלֵ֜י⁠נוּ הָ⁠אָלָ֣ה וְ⁠הַ⁠שְּׁבֻעָ֗ה

**"And the oath has been poured out on us, and the curse"**

Daniel is speaking as if the oath and the curse were a liquid that could be **poured out** on the Israelites. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And we have experienced the oath and the curse]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:11
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Daniel 9:11 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_rjrr)

וַ⁠תִּתַּ֨ךְ עָלֵ֜י⁠נוּ הָ⁠אָלָ֣ה

**"And the oath has been poured out on us"**

The expression **has been poured out** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[And you have poured out the oath]

See: Active or Passive

אֲשֶׁ֤ר כְּתוּבָה֙

**"that is written"**

The expression **that is written** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[that God had written]

## Daniel 9:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_a4mv)

וַ⁠יָּ֜קֶם אֶת־דברי⁠ו

**"And he has made stand his word"**

Daniel is using **made stand his word** as a common expression of the culture to mean “fulfilled his word.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[kept his word] or \[done what he said he would do]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:12
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Daniel 9:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_wg51)

אֲשֶׁ֣ר לֹֽא־נֶעֶשְׂתָ֗ה תַּ֚חַת כָּל־הַ⁠שָּׁמַ֔יִם כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁ֥ר נֶעֶשְׂתָ֖ה בִּ⁠ירוּשָׁלִָֽם

**"that has not been done under all of the heavens like what has been done to Jerusalem"**

The expressions **has not been done** and **has been done** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[that he has not done anywhere under all of the heavens like what he has done to Jerusalem]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:12
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Daniel 9:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_km80)

וְ⁠לֹֽא־חִלִּ֜ינוּ אֶת־פְּנֵ֣י ׀ יְהוָ֣ה אֱלֹהֵ֗י⁠נוּ

**"but we have not entreated the face of Yahweh our God"**

Daniel is using **the face** to represent Yahweh himself. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression from your culture or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[but we have not entreated Yahweh our God himself]

See: Synecdoche

## Daniel 9:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_xja1)

לָ⁠שׁוּב֙ מֵֽ⁠עֲוֺנֵ֔⁠נוּ

**"to return from our iniquities"**

Daniel is speaking as if abandoning sin were **to return from** a place where the people had been. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[to turn away from our iniquities] or \[to repent of our sin]

## Daniel 9:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_acx8)

וַ⁠יִּשְׁקֹ֤ד יְהוָה֙ עַל־הָ֣⁠רָעָ֔ה

**"And Yahweh watched over the trouble"**

Daniel is using **Yahweh watched over the trouble** as a common expression of the culture to mean “Yahweh kept the disaster ready to send.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And Yahweh watched for the right time to bring the disaster]

## Daniel 9:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_t9iw)

וְ⁠עַתָּ֣ה ׀ אֲדֹנָ֣⁠י אֱלֹהֵ֗י⁠נוּ

**"And now, Lord our God"**

The words **And now** mark a transition from Daniel’s confession to his request. If the connection between this statement and the previous one is not clear, you may want to use a different connecting word to show how this statement relates to what comes before it. Alternate translation: \[So then, Lord our God]

See: Connecting Words and Phrases

## Daniel 9:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_v77o)

כְּ⁠כָל־צִדְקֹתֶ֨⁠ךָ֙

**"according to all of your righteousnesses"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **righteousnesses**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[according to your righteous deeds]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:16
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`)

## Daniel 9:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_q5r2)

כְּ⁠כָל־צִדְקֹתֶ֨⁠ךָ֙

**"according to all of your righteousnesses"**

The phrase **according to all of your righteousnesses** probably refers initially to Yahweh’s “righteous acts,” that is, his acts of deliverance, supremely in the Exodus, described in v. 15\. But in light of the overall prayer, it also refers to his righteous character (vv. 7, 14\). This phrase could mean: (1\) \[in keeping with your acts of deliverance] or (2\) \[because you always do what is right]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:16
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`)

## Daniel 9:16 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_pth0)

כְּ⁠כָל־צִדְקֹתֶ֨⁠ךָ֙

**"according to all of your righteousnesses"**

The implication is that **your righteousnesses** are the righteous deeds by which Yahweh had rescued his people in the past. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[according to all of the righteous deeds by which you have rescued us in the past]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

יָֽשָׁב־נָ֤א אַפְּ⁠ךָ֙ וַ⁠חֲמָ֣תְ⁠ךָ֔

**"may your nose and your heat please turn back"**

Daniel is using **your nose and your heat** as a common expression of the culture to mean “your burning anger.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[may you please turn your fierce anger away]

Daniel is using the possessive form to describe a **mountain** that is characterized by **holiness**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[your holy mountain]

הַר־קָדְשֶׁ֑⁠ךָ

**"the mountain of your holiness"**

## Daniel 9:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_b0oy)

אֶל־תְּפִלַּ֤ת עַבְדְּ⁠ךָ֙ וְ⁠אֶל־תַּ֣חֲנוּנָ֔י⁠ו

**"to the prayer of your servant and to his supplications"**

Even though Daniel is speaking about himself, he refers to himself in the third person as **your servant** to show humility before God. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[listen to my prayer, your servant, and to my supplications]

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:17
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_DAN_ntbk)

וְ⁠הָאֵ֣ר פָּנֶ֔י⁠ךָ עַל־מִקְדָּשְׁ⁠ךָ֖ הַ⁠שָּׁמֵ֑ם

**"and make your face shine on your desolate sanctuary"**

Daniel is speaking as if Yahweh’s favor were light shining from his **face** onto the sanctuary. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and show your favor to your desolate sanctuary]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:17
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

לְמַ֖עַן אֲדֹנָֽ⁠י

**"for the sake of the Lord"**

Even though Daniel is addressing Yahweh directly, he is speaking of him in the third person as **the Lord** to show respect. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in a respectful form in the second person. Alternate translation: \[for your own sake, my Lord]

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:17
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_DAN_zwg7)

הַטֵּ֨ה אֱלֹהַ֥⁠י ׀ אָזְנְ⁠ךָ֮ וּֽ⁠שֲׁמָע֒

**"Incline your ear, my God, and hear"**

Daniel is using **Incline your ear** as a common expression of the culture to mean “please give me your attention.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Pay attention, my God, and hear]

Daniel is using **Open your eyes** as a common expression of the culture to mean “pay attention.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Daniel 9:18 (#3) (id: en_tn_DAN_ew3e)

שֹֽׁמְמֹתֵ֔י⁠נוּ וְ⁠הָ⁠עִ֕יר אֲשֶׁר־נִקְרָ֥א שִׁמְ⁠ךָ֖ עָלֶ֑י⁠הָ

**"our desolations and the city upon which your name is called"**

The phrase **our desolations and the city upon which your name is called** expresses a single idea. The word **desolations** describes the state of **the city**. If it would be more natural in your language, you could express this meaning in a different way. Alternate translation: \[the desolations of our city, upon which your name is called] or \[the city upon which your name is called, which lies in desolation]

See: Hendiadys

* **Associated Passages:** Daniel 9:18
* **Associated Articles:** Hendiadys (ID: `33280@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Daniel 9:18 (#4) (id: en_tn_DAN_rldb)

**"and the city upon which your name is called"**

The expression **your name is called** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and the city upon which you have put your name]
